# Modified realizability subtoposes and total Weihrauch reducibility

**arXiv ID**: 2608.02133v1
**Authors**: Akihito Kajikawa, Masamori Kaku, Takayuki Kihara, Satoshi Nakata
**Published**: 2026-08-03
**Categories**: math.LO, cs.LO
**HTML URL**: https://arxiv.org/html/2608.02133v1

## Abstract

In recent years, there has been rapid development in the foundational study of oracle computability from the perspective of Lawvere-Tierney topologies and their sheaves. In this article, we formulate and analyze the notion of reducibility within the framework of total computability. Then, using sheaf subtoposes derived from oracles in the total computable setting, we establish separations between various hierarchies of logical principles, including the hierarchies of the weak law of excluded middle $\mathbf{WLEM}$, the lessor limited principle of omniscience $\mathbf{LLPO}$, and Markov's principle $\mathbf{MP}$.

## Modified realizability subtoposes and total Weihrauch reducibilityAkihito Kajikawa,Masamori Kaku,Takayuki KiharaandSatoshi Nakata

## Abstract.

In recent years, there has been rapid development in the foundational study of oracle computability from the perspective of Lawvere-Tierney topologies and their sheaves.
In this article, we formulate and analyze the notion of reducibility within the framework of total computability.
Then, using sheaf subtoposes derived from oracles in the total computable setting, we establish separations between various hierarchies of logical principles, including the hierarchies of the weak law of excluded middle𝐖𝐋𝐄𝐌\mathbf{WLEM}, the lessor limited principle of omniscience𝐋𝐋𝐏𝐎\mathbf{LLPO}, and Markov’s principle𝐌𝐏\mathbf{MP}.Graduate School of Informatics and Graduate School of Mathematics, Nagoya University, Japan

## 1.Introduction

## 1.1.Summary

One of the most fundamental areas of study in computability theory is the investigation of the degrees of difficulty of various problems; this requires the notion of oracle computability to formalize the comparison of such problems.
In recent years, a new theoretical framework for oracle computability has been rapidly developing, based on notions from topos theory – specifically, Lawvere-Tierney (LT) topologies and their associated sheaves.

At an early stage, Hyland[16]pointed out that Turing oracles (single-valued oracles) could be viewed as LT-topologies on the effective topos, yet subsequent development of this perspective was rather slow; see e.g.[37].
However, in recent years, this field has seen rapid development, driven by itsco-development with concrete theory.
Key turning points include the concrete presentation and combinatorial analysis of LT-topologies on the effective topos by Lee-van Oosten[25], and the connection established by Kihara[24]to the concrete theory of Weihrauch degrees[6]that has been extensively studied in computable analysis.
This evolution has also shifted the primary research focus fromsingle-valued (deterministic) oraclestomulti-valued (non-deterministic) oracles.

There are numerous important points regarding the new foundation for oracle computability.
- A.

First, the abstraction led to the discovery of notions that appropriately extend the standard notion of oracle computability:
the extended Weihrauch reducibility by Bauer[4]and its multi-query version by Kihara[24], known as the Arthur-Nimue-Merlin reducibility.
This incorporates not onlydemonicnon-determinism but alsoangelicnon-determinism; see also Abou Samra-Madore[1].
- –

As a byproduct, Kihara-Ng[20]incorporated “computability by majority” as a type of oracle computability and discovered connections to existing research on the strength of ultrafilters and ideals (i.e., the Rudin-Keisler order and the Katětov order).
- B.

Next, based on extended oracle computability, it becomes possible to perform the concrete analysis of the structure of LT-topologies and associated sheaf subtoposes on the effective topos; see e.g. Kihara[24], Kihara-Ng[19], Kihara-Ng[20,21].
- C.

Furthermore, the abstraction allows for a unified treatment of various oracle notions – such as not only oracles for partial computability (the standard setting in computability theory) but also oracles for total computability, which is the subject of this article.
- D.

Finally, a LT-topology induces a sheaf subtopos of a base topos, and a topos provides a certain kind of mathematical universe.
Consequently, oracle computability automatically yields a mathematical model; by utilizing this, one can achieve unprovability proofs relying solely on arguments concerning oracle computability/reducibility.
See e.g. Kihara[22], Kihara-Ng[18], and Nakata[28,29]

To demonstrate the power of this idea, this article addresses the problem of separating the hierarchy of logical principles as a test case.
We deal here with the hierarchy of the weak law of excluded middle𝐖𝐋𝐄𝐌¯\underline{{\bf WLEM}}, the lessor limited principle of omniscience𝐋𝐋𝐏𝐎¯\underline{{\bf LLPO}}, and Markov’s principle𝐌𝐏¯\underline{{\bf MP}}.
These are also known as the propositional de Morgan’s law,Σ1\Sigma_{1}-de Morgan’s law, and theΣ1\Sigma_{1}-double negation elimination principle, respectively.
The disjunctive Markov’s principle𝐌𝐏¯∨\underline{{\bf MP}}^{\lor}is also known as theΠ1\Pi_{1}-de Morgan’s law.

We provide a complete separation of the principles appearing in the following figure:𝐋𝐄𝐌¯{\underline{{\bf LEM}}}𝐌𝐏¯{\underline{{\bf MP}}}𝐖𝐋𝐄𝐌¯{\underline{{\bf WLEM}}}𝐋𝐋𝐏𝐎¯{\underline{{\bf LLPO}}}𝐌𝐏¯∨{\underline{{\bf MP}}^{\vee}}𝐖𝐋𝐄𝐌¯n{\underline{{\bf WLEM}}_{n}}𝐋𝐋𝐏𝐎¯n{\underline{{\bf LLPO}}_{n}}𝐌𝐏¯n∨{\underline{{\bf MP}}^{\vee}_{n}}𝐖𝐋𝐄𝐌¯ω{\underline{{\bf WLEM}}_{\omega}}𝐋𝐋𝐏𝐎¯ω{\underline{{\bf LLPO}}_{\omega}}𝐌𝐏¯ω∨{\underline{{\bf MP}}^{\vee}_{\omega}}

The precise definitions of these principles are given in Section7.
Similar separations have also been achieved by Hendtlass-Lubarsky[14].
On the one hand, their separation models are combinations of Kripke semantics with set-theoretic models.
On the other hand, our separation models are certain kinds of realizability models based on the connection between oracle computability and LT topology, thereby achieving separation while satisfying several variants of constructive Church’s thesis𝐂𝐓¯0\underline{{\bf CT}}_{0}(every total relation has a computable choice).
The variants we consider include𝐂𝐓¯0!\underline{{\bf CT}}_{0}!(every total function is computable),𝐰𝐂𝐓¯0\underline{{\bf wCT}}_{0}(a double negated variant of Church’s thesis),𝐂𝐓¯0maj\underline{{\bf CT}}_{0}^{\rm maj}(every total relation has a computable bound).
For the precise definitions, see Sections7.4and7.6.

Here,𝐌𝐏¯PR\underline{{\bf MP}}_{\rm PR}is a primitive recursive variant of𝐌𝐏¯\underline{{\bf MP}}.

In addition, our models for (1), (5), (6), (8) and (9) also satisfy the independence of premise𝐈𝐏¯\underline{{\bf IP}}(see Section7.7).
While our model for (12) does not satisfy any of the variants of Church’s thesis mentioned so far, it does satisfy some weakened principle𝐬𝐂𝐓¯0maj\underline{{\bf sCT}}_{0}^{\rm maj}(every total relation has a lower semicomputable bound; see Section7.6).

## Remark.

Each of the items (4), (7), (10), (11), and (12) can be satisfied even in a subtopos of the effective topos, but the others cannot (since they refute𝐌𝐏¯\underline{{\bf MP}}).

The proof of this theorem utilizes all of the key points A., B., C. and D. mentioned above.
- A.

An extra axiom in logic translates to an oracle in computability theory.
However, the hierarchy of the weak law of excluded middle𝐖𝐋𝐄𝐌¯n\underline{{\bf WLEM}}_{n}does not fit within the framework of the standard notion of oracle.
This issue is resolved by an extended notion of oracle (Section4), which was recently discovered through the aforementioned abstract theory.
- B.

The analysis of the strength of oracles (i.e., the degree structure) is reflected in the analysis of the strength of LT topologies (Section6).
Our proof begins by establishing separations in the strength of oracles (Section5).
All separations are achieved using crystal-clear arguments familiar to computability theorists.
- C.

Markov’s principle𝐌𝐏¯\underline{{\bf MP}}is a partial computable principle (i.e., realizable by an unbounded search), so the oracle computability based on standard partial computability cannot distinguish the strength of variants𝐌𝐏¯n∨\underline{{\bf MP}}_{n}^{\lor}of Markov’s principle.
We achieve separations of variants of Markov’s principle by newly introducing notions of oracle and reducibility based on total computability.
This new notion of reducibility is introduced automatically from the abstract theory (Sections2and3).
- D.

Each oracle induces an LT-topology, which in turn determines a sheaf subtopos (Section6).
Through this abstract framework, the separation of oracle strengths is translated into the construction of models separating logical principles (Section7).

## 1.2.Contents

In Section2, we introduce the notion of total Weihrauch reducibility, which a notion of one-query reducibility with respect to total computability.
We also reformulate various logical principles as computability-theoretic problems – specifically, as choice problems – and investigate the one-query reducibility relations among them.
In Section3, we introduce the notion of total multi-query reducibility and investigate its fundamental properties.
In Section4, we generalize this idea to the setting of extended oracles, i.e., bilayer problems.
In Section5, we establish separations of strength for various selection problems with respect to total multi-query reducibility.
In Section6, we clarify the precise relationship between an oracle and a LT-topology, and between total computable realizability with multiple queries to an oracle and the semantics of the correspoding sheaf subtopos on the topos for total computability (the Grayson topos).
Section7, we then demonstrate the precise correspondence between logical principles and selection problems, thereby linking logic and computability.
By combining these results, we establish the main theorem in Section7.8.

For the basics of computability theory, we refer the reader to[30].
For the basics of constructive mathematics, we refer the reader to[5,7].
For the basics of realizability theory, we refer the reader to[34].

In this article, we allow the use of classical principles for the meta-logic.
However, with careful argumentation, the use of classical principles can be kept to a necessary minimum.

## 2.One-Query Computability

## 2.1.Total Weihrauch reducibility

The primary subject of study in computability theory is the analysis of the complexity of problems.
Here, a “problem” is actually a collection of problems, which is often displayed as a partial multifunctionF:⊆X⇉YF\colon\!\!\subseteq X\rightrightarrows Y.
An inputx∈dom​(F)x\in\mathrm{dom}(F)is called aninstanceof the problemFF, and eachy∈F​(x)y\in F(x)is a solution to this instance.
That is,F​(x)F(x)is the solution set for thexxth instance of the problemFF.

In computability theory, we typically do not require information about the underlying typeXX, but only requires information aboutdom​(F){\rm dom}(F).
However, in the theory of total computability that we are dealing with, we require information about the typeXXto define the notion of totality.
Therefore, we treat the notion of a problem as a data equipped with the typeXXof potential instances.

We assume that all objects are encoded by natural numbers, so formally, we introduce the notion of a problem as follows.

## Definition 2.1.

Apotted problemis a triple(X,Y;F)(X,Y;F)consisting of setsX,Y⊆ωX,Y\subseteq\omegacontaining0and a subsetF⊆X×YF\subseteq X\times Y.

We use the following notations and terminologies:
- •

We writeF:⊆X⇉YF\colon\!\!\subseteq X\rightrightarrows Yfor such a triple.
- •

Such anXXis denoted bydom+​(F){\rm dom}_{+}(F), which is called thepotential domain(or thep.domain).
- •

Such anYYis denoted bycod+​(F){\rm cod}_{+}(F), which is called thepotential codomain(or thep.codomain)
- •

For eachx∈Xx\in X,F​(x)F(x)is defined as{y∈Y:(x,y)∈F}\{y\in Y:(x,y)\in F\}.
- •

dom​(F){\rm dom}(F)is defined as{x∈X:F​(x)≠∅}\{x\in X:F(x)\not=\emptyset\}, which is called theactual domain(or thea.domain).
- •

IfF​(x)F(x)is a singleton{y}\{y\}, then we writeF​(x)=yF(x)=y.
- •

IfF​(x)F(x)is a singleton for anyx∈dom​(F)x\in{\rm dom}(F), then we say thatFFissingle-valuedand writeF:⊆X→YF\colon\!\!\subseteq X\to Y.

Note that under this formulation,FFis always a nonempty-valued.
(Although some approaches allow for empty-valued problems, this article does not deal with such problems.)
In later sections, we will frequently use the following notion to deal with the domain of a potted problem.

## Definition 2.2.

Apotted setis a pairA¯=(A−;A+)\underline{A}=(A_{-};A_{+})such thatA−⊆A+⊆ωA_{-}\subseteq A_{+}\subseteq\omegaand0∈A+0\in A_{+}.

This is a structure required to formalize the notion of a “total function onAA.”
The underlying set itself isA−A_{-}, but it is necessary to specify the scope oftotality, and that is preciselyA+A_{+}.
That is, we think of a potted set as a setA−A_{-}equipped with the scopeA+A_{+}of totality.
This notion is inspired by Grayson’s version of modified assembly/tripos[36].
The details will be explained in Section6.

## Notation.

IfX¯=(X−;X+)\underline{X}=(X_{-};X_{+})is a potted set, we also use the notationF:X¯⇉YF\colon\underline{X}\rightrightarrows Yfor a potted problemFFsuch thatdom+​(F)=X+{\rm dom}_{+}(F)=X_{+}anddom​(F)=X−{\rm dom}(F)=X_{-}.

LetFFandGGbe potted problems.
We say thatFFistotal Weihrauch reducible toGGif there are partial computable functionsν,ψ\nu,\psisuch that the following hold:
- (1)

ν:dom+​(F)→dom+​(G)\nu\colon{\rm dom}_{+}(F)\to{\rm dom}_{+}(G)is total.
- (2)

ψ:dom+​(F)×cod+​(G)→cod+​(F)\psi\colon{\rm dom}_{+}(F)\times{\rm cod}_{+}(G)\to{\rm cod}_{+}(F)is total.
- (3)

x∈dom​(F)x\in{\rm dom}(F)impliesν​(x)∈dom​(G)\nu(x)\in{\rm dom}(G).
- (4)

x∈dom​(F)x\in{\rm dom}(F)andy∈G​(ν​(x))y\in G(\nu(x))implyψ​(x,y)∈F​(x)\psi(x,y)\in F(x).

In this case, we writeF≤𝗍𝖶GF\leq_{\sf tW}G.
We callν\nuaninner reductionandψ\psianouter reduction.

The difference from the standard Weihrauch reduction[6]is that Definition2.3requires totality on thep.domains/codomains.

## 2.2.Weihrauch Problems

We investigate the total Weihrauch strength of various potted problems.
We use the following notations:
- •

Letφe\varphi_{e}denote theeeth partial computable function onω\omega.
- •

LetSeS_{e}be theΣ1\Sigma_{1}set coded byee; that is,Se={n∈ω:φe(n)↓}S_{e}=\{n\in\omega:\varphi_{e}(n)\downarrow\}.
- •

LetPeP_{e}be theΠ1\Pi_{1}set coded byee; that is,Pe={n∈ω:φe(n)↑}P_{e}=\{n\in\omega:\varphi_{e}(n)\uparrow\}.

## 2.2.1.Σ1\Sigma_{1}-Choice

AΣ1\Sigma_{1}-choiceproblem is a problem in which, given a code for a non-emptyΣ1\Sigma_{1}setSeS_{e}, one is asked to pick up an elementn∈Sen\in S_{e}.
AΣ1\Sigma_{1}-choice problem is computably solvable by anunbounded search, and is therefore trivial in the context of the standard Weihrauch reducibility.
However, since an unbounded search is not total computable,Σ1\Sigma_{1}-choice becomes non-trivial in the context of total Weihrauch reducibility.

## Definition 2.5.

For0<k<n≤ω0<k<n\leq\omega, theΣ1\Sigma_{1}-choice problemΣ1​-​𝖢k/n\Sigma_{1}\text{-}{\sf C}_{k/n}is defined as follows:
- •

dom+​(Σ1​-​𝖢k/n)=ω{\rm dom}_{+}(\Sigma_{1}\text{-}{\sf C}_{k/n})=\omega, andcod+​(Σ1​-​𝖢k/n)=n{\rm cod}_{+}(\Sigma_{1}\text{-}{\sf C}_{k/n})=n.
- •

dom​(Σ1​-​𝖢k/n)={e∈ω:|Se|=k}{\rm dom}(\Sigma_{1}\text{-}{\sf C}_{k/n})=\{e\in\omega:|S_{e}|=k\}.
- •

Σ1​-​𝖢k/n​(e)={k<n:k∈Se}\Sigma_{1}\text{-}{\sf C}_{k/n}(e)=\{k<n:k\in S_{e}\}.

## Definition 2.6.

For0<k<n≤ω0<k<n\leq\omega, theΣ1\Sigma_{1}-choice problemΣ1​-​𝖢≥k/n\Sigma_{1}\text{-}{\sf C}_{\geq k/n}is defined as follows:
- •

dom+​(Σ1​-​𝖢≥k/n)=ω{\rm dom}_{+}(\Sigma_{1}\text{-}{\sf C}_{\geq k/n})=\omega, andcod+​(Σ1​-​𝖢k/n)=n{\rm cod}_{+}(\Sigma_{1}\text{-}{\sf C}_{k/n})=n.
- •

dom​(Σ1​-​𝖢≥k/n)={e∈ω:|Se|≥k}{\rm dom}(\Sigma_{1}\text{-}{\sf C}_{\geq k/n})=\{e\in\omega:|S_{e}|\geq k\}.
- •

Σ1​-​𝖢≥k/n​(e)={k<n:k∈Se}\Sigma_{1}\text{-}{\sf C}_{\geq k/n}(e)=\{k<n:k\in S_{e}\}.

In words: There arennoptions, exactly/at leastkkof which are correct.
Given a code of an enumeration of correct elements, the problem asks us to pick up one of them.

One may defineΠ1​-​𝖢k/n\Pi_{1}\text{-}{\sf C}_{k/n}andΠ1​-​𝖢≥k/n\Pi_{1}\text{-}{\sf C}_{\geq k/n}in the same manner.
We call𝖢≥1/n{\sf C}_{\geq 1/n}thechoiceproblem,𝖢1/n{\sf C}_{1/n}theunique choiceproblem,𝖢n−1/n{\sf C}_{n-1/n}theco-unique choiceproblem, and𝖢≥n−1/n{\sf C}_{\geq n-1/n}theall-or-co-uniqueproblem.

With a slight modification to the definition, the notion of co-unique choice also makes sense forn=ωn=\omega.

## Definition 2.7.

For0<k<n≤ω0<k<n\leq\omega, theΣ1\Sigma_{1}-choice problemΣ1​-​𝖢n−k/n\Sigma_{1}\text{-}{\sf C}_{n-k/n}is defined as follows:
- •

dom+​(Σ1​-​𝖢n−k/n)=ω{\rm dom}_{+}(\Sigma_{1}\text{-}{\sf C}_{n-k/n})=\omega, andcod+​(Σ1​-​𝖢n−k/n)=n{\rm cod}_{+}(\Sigma_{1}\text{-}{\sf C}_{n-k/n})=n.
- •

dom​(Σ1​-​𝖢n−k/n)={e∈ω:|n∖Se|=k}{\rm dom}(\Sigma_{1}\text{-}{\sf C}_{n-k/n})=\{e\in\omega:|n\setminus S_{e}|=k\}.
- •

Σ1​-​𝖢n−k/n​(e)={k<n:k∈Se}\Sigma_{1}\text{-}{\sf C}_{n-k/n}(e)=\{k<n:k\in S_{e}\}.

## Definition 2.8.

For0<k<n≤ω0<k<n\leq\omega, theΣ1\Sigma_{1}-choice problemΣ1​-​𝖢≥n−k/n\Sigma_{1}\text{-}{\sf C}_{\geq n-k/n}is defined as follows:
- •

dom+​(Σ1​-​𝖢≥n−k/n)=ω{\rm dom}_{+}(\Sigma_{1}\text{-}{\sf C}_{\geq n-k/n})=\omega, andcod+​(Σ1​-​𝖢≥n−k/n)=n{\rm cod}_{+}(\Sigma_{1}\text{-}{\sf C}_{\geq n-k/n})=n.
- •

dom​(Σ1​-​𝖢≥n−k/n)={e∈ω:|n∖Se|≤k}{\rm dom}(\Sigma_{1}\text{-}{\sf C}_{\geq n-k/n})=\{e\in\omega:|n\setminus S_{e}|\leq k\}.
- •

Σ1​-​𝖢≥n−k/n​(e)={k<n:k∈Se}\Sigma_{1}\text{-}{\sf C}_{\geq n-k/n}(e)=\{k<n:k\in S_{e}\}.

For instance,𝖢ω−1/ω{\sf C}_{\omega-1/\omega}stands for the co-unique choice onω\omega.

## Observation 2.9.

LetΓ∈{Σ1,Π1}\Gamma\in\{\Sigma_{1},\Pi_{1}\}.
Forℓ≤k\ell\leq kandn≤mn\leq m:Γ​-​𝖢k/n≤𝗍𝖶Γ​-​𝖢ℓ/m;\displaystyle\Gamma\text{-}{\sf C}_{k/n}\leq_{\sf tW}\Gamma\text{-}{\sf C}_{\ell/m};Γ​-​𝖢≥k/n≤𝗍𝖶Γ​-​𝖢≥ℓ/m;\displaystyle\Gamma\text{-}{\sf C}_{\geq k/n}\leq_{\sf tW}\Gamma\text{-}{\sf C}_{\geq\ell/m};Γ​-​𝖢k/n≤𝗍𝖶Γ​-​𝖢≥k/n.\displaystyle\Gamma\text{-}{\sf C}_{k/n}\leq_{\sf tW}\Gamma\text{-}{\sf C}_{\geq k/n}.

In particular, the following relationship generally holds among the types of choice problems (for a fixed number of options):unique choice≤𝗍𝖶\scriptstyle{\leq_{\sf tW}}co-unique choice≤𝗍𝖶\scriptstyle{\leq_{\sf tW}}≤𝗍𝖶\scriptstyle{\leq_{\sf tW}}choiceall-or-co-unique choice≤𝗍𝖶\scriptstyle{\leq_{\sf tW}}

Note here that the direction of the arrows is the reverse of that for the logical entailment relationship; that is, “choice” is the strongest, and “co-unique choice” is the weakest.

We show that theΣ1\Sigma_{1}-choice problem and theΣ1\Sigma_{1}-unique-choice problem have the same strength.

## Proposition 2.10.

Σ1​-​𝖢1/n≡𝗍𝖶Σ1​-​𝖢≥1/n\Sigma_{1}\text{-}{\sf C}_{1/n}\equiv_{\sf tW}\Sigma_{1}\text{-}{\sf C}_{\geq 1/n}for anyn≤ωn\leq\omega.

Σ1​-​𝖢1/n≤𝗍𝖶Σ1​-​𝖢≥1/n\Sigma_{1}\text{-}{\sf C}_{1/n}\leq_{\sf tW}\Sigma_{1}\text{-}{\sf C}_{\geq 1/n}: By Observation2.9.

Σ1​-​𝖢≥1/n≤𝗍𝖶Σ1​-​𝖢1/n\Sigma_{1}\text{-}{\sf C}_{\geq 1/n}\leq_{\sf tW}\Sigma_{1}\text{-}{\sf C}_{1/n}:
We only deal with the casen=ωn=\omega.

Inner reduction.For eache∈ωe\in\omega, consider the following algorithmν​(e)\nu(e):

Declareφν​(e)​(k)↓\varphi_{\nu(e)}(k){\downarrow}only for the first found suchkk.
Note that, even ifφν​(e)​(k)\varphi_{\nu(e)}(k)is undefined, the algorithmν​(e)\nu(e)itself is defined, soν\nuis total.
- •

Outer reduction.For eache,k∈ωe,k\in\omega, putψ​(e,k)=k\psi(e,k)=k.
Clearly,ψ\psiis total.

Verification:
- •

Inner reduction.For each instancee∈dom​(Σ1​-​𝖢≥1/ω)e\in{\rm dom}(\Sigma_{1}\text{-}{\sf C}_{\geq 1/\omega}), theΣ1\Sigma_{1}setSe={k∈ω:φe(k)↓}⊆ωS_{e}=\{k\in\omega:\varphi_{e}(k)\downarrow\}\subseteq\omegais nonempty.
Then, our definition ensuresφν​(e)​(k)↓\varphi_{\nu(e)}(k){\downarrow}for a uniquekk, soSν​(e)⊆SeS_{\nu(e)}\subseteq S_{e}is a singleton.
Therefore, we haveν​(e)∈dom​(Σ1​-​𝖢1/ω)\nu(e)\in{\rm dom}(\Sigma_{1}\text{-}{\sf C}_{1/\omega}).

We show that theΠ1\Pi_{1}-co-unique choice problem, theΣ1\Sigma_{1}-co-unique choice problem, and theΣ1\Sigma_{1}-all-or-co-unique choice problem have the same strength.

## Theorem 2.11.

Π1​-​𝖢n−1/n≡𝗍𝖶Σ1​-​𝖢n−1/n≡𝗍𝖶Σ1​-​𝖢≥n−1/n\Pi_{1}\text{-}{\sf C}_{n-1/n}\equiv_{\sf tW}\Sigma_{1}\text{-}{\sf C}_{n-1/n}\equiv_{\sf tW}\Sigma_{1}\text{-}{\sf C}_{\geq n-1/n}for anyn≤ωn\leq\omega.

Inner reduction.Consider the following algorithmν​(e)\nu(e):

Then there isk<nk<nsuch thatPe=ω∖{k}P_{e}=\omega\setminus\{k\}; that is,φe​(k)↓\varphi_{e}(k){\downarrow}.

In particular,Sν​(e)S_{\nu(e)}is a co-singleton, so we haveν​(e)∈dom​(Σ1​-​𝖢n−1/n)\nu(e)\in{\rm dom}(\Sigma_{1}\text{-}{\sf C}_{n-1/n}).
- •

Outer reduction.For each solutionv∈Σ1​-​𝖢n−1/n​(ν​(e))=Sν​(e)v\in\Sigma_{1}\text{-}{\sf C}_{n-1/n}(\nu(e))=S_{\nu(e)}, we haveψ​(e,v)=v∈Sν​(e)=Pe\psi(e,v)=v\in S_{\nu(e)}=P_{e}.

Σ1​-​𝖢n−1/n≤𝗍𝖶Σ1​-​𝖢≥n−1/n\Sigma_{1}\text{-}{\sf C}_{n-1/n}\leq_{\sf tW}\Sigma_{1}\text{-}{\sf C}_{\geq n-1/n}:
By Observation2.9.

Σ1​-​𝖢≥n−1/n≤𝗍𝖶Π1​-​𝖢n−1/n\Sigma_{1}\text{-}{\sf C}_{\geq n-1/n}\leq_{\sf tW}\Pi_{1}\text{-}{\sf C}_{n-1/n}forn<ωn<\omega:
- •

Inner reduction.Consider the following algorithmν​(e)\nu(e):
Wait until seeingφe​(j)↓\varphi_{e}(j){\downarrow}for allj<nj<nexcept for one input.
If this happens, for the uniquekkfor whichφe​(k)\varphi_{e}(k)is still undefined, declareφν​(e)​(k)↓\varphi_{\nu(e)}(k){\downarrow}.
For all other inputsk<nk<n,φν​(e)​(k)\varphi_{\nu(e)}(k)does not halt.
Again, the algorithmν​(e)\nu(e)itself is always defined, soν\nuis total.
- •

Outer reduction.Givene,v∈ωe,v\in\omega, putψ​(e,v)=v\psi(e,v)=v.
Clearly,ψ\psiis total.

Verification:
- •

Inner reduction.For each instancee∈dom​(Σ1​-​𝖢≥n−1/n)e\in{\rm dom}(\Sigma_{1}\text{-}{\sf C}_{\geq n-1/n}), we have|n∖Se|≤1|n\setminus S_{e}|\leq 1.
Hence, once we havej∈Sej\in S_{e}for allj<nj<nexcept for one value ofk<nk<n, we obtainPν​(e)={j<n:φν​(e)(j)↑}=ω∖{k}⊆SeP_{\nu(e)}=\{j<n:\varphi_{\nu(e)}(j){\uparrow}\}=\omega\setminus\{k\}\subseteq S_{e}.
In particular,Pν​(e)P_{\nu(e)}is a co-singleton, so we haveν​(e)∈dom​(Π1​-​𝖢n−1/n)\nu(e)\in{\rm dom}(\Pi_{1}\text{-}{\sf C}_{n-1/n}).
- •

Outer reduction.For each solutionv∈Π1​-​𝖢n−1/n​(ν​(e))=Pν​(e)v\in\Pi_{1}\text{-}{\sf C}_{n-1/n}(\nu(e))=P_{\nu(e)}, we haveψ​(e,v)=v∈Sν​(e)=Pe\psi(e,v)=v\in S_{\nu(e)}=P_{e}.

Σ1​-​𝖢≥ω−1/ω≤𝗍𝖶Π1​-​𝖢ω−1/ω\Sigma_{1}\text{-}{\sf C}_{\geq\omega-1/\omega}\leq_{\sf tW}\Pi_{1}\text{-}{\sf C}_{\omega-1/\omega}:
In the case ofω\omega, a slightly different argument is required.
Consider the following total reductions:
- •

Inner reduction.Consider the following algorithmν​(e)\nu(e):
Wait for a stagettsuch that somekkis enumerated intoSeS_{e}.
Letttbe the first stage.
Then wait until all elements less thantt, except for at most one elementu<tu<t, are enumerated intoSeS_{e}.
If this happens, we removeuufromPν​(e)P_{\nu(e)}; that is,Pν​(e)=ω∖{u}P_{\nu(e)}=\omega\setminus\{u\}.
If this never happens, we havePν​(e)=ωP_{\nu(e)}=\omega.
The algorithmν​(e)\nu(e)is explicitly defined, soν\nuis total.
- •

Outer reduction.For eache,v∈ωe,v\in\omega, if no elements have been enumerated intoSeS_{e}by stagevv, then putψ​(e,v)=v\psi(e,v)=v.
Otherwise, for the first such elementk∈Sek\in S_{e}, putψ​(e,v)=k\psi(e,v)=k.
Since the valueψ​(e,v)\psi(e,v)is always determined,ψ\psiis total.

Verification:
- •

Inner reduction.For each instancee∈dom​(Σ1​-​𝖢≥ω−1/ω)e\in{\rm dom}(\Sigma_{1}\text{-}{\sf C}_{\geq\omega-1/\omega}), some elementkkmust be enumerated intoSeS_{e}at some stagett.
Moreover, all elements less thantt, except for at most one elementu<tu<t, are enumerated intoSeS_{e}by some stage (it is possible thatuuwill be enumerated intoSeS_{e}at a later stage).
Then our construction ensuresPν​(e)=ω∖{u}P_{\nu(e)}=\omega\setminus\{u\}, soPν​(e)P_{\nu(e)}is a co-singleton.
Hence. we haveν​(e)∈dom​(Π1​-​𝖢ω−1/ω)\nu(e)\in{\rm dom}(\Pi_{1}\text{-}{\sf C}_{\omega-1/\omega}).
- •

Outer reduction.For each solutionv∈Π1​-​𝖢ω−1/ω​(ν​(e))=Pν​(e)v\in\Pi_{1}\text{-}{\sf C}_{\omega-1/\omega}(\nu(e))=P_{\nu(e)}, ifv≥tv\geq tthen we haveψ​(e,v)=k∈Se\psi(e,v)=k\in S_{e}.
Forv<tv<t, ifv∈Pν​(e)v\in P_{\nu(e)}thenv≠uv\not=u, and by our choice ofuu,vvis enumerated intoSeS_{e}beforeuu.
Hence,ψ​(e,v)=v∈Se\psi(e,v)=v\in S_{e}.
In any case, we getψ​(e,v)∈Se=Σ1​-​𝖢≥ω−1/ω​(e)\psi(e,v)\in S_{e}=\Sigma_{1}\text{-}{\sf C}_{\geq\omega-1/\omega}(e).

∎

All of theΣ1\Sigma_{1}-choice problems discussed so far are computably solvable using unbounded search.
Therefore, the complexity of these choice problems is all trivial in terms of Weihrauch degrees.
Interestingly, when considering the𝗍𝖶{\sf tW}-degrees, we will get the following sequence ofΣ1\Sigma_{1}-choice problems:(⋆\star)Σ1​-​𝖢ω−1/ω<𝗍𝖶⋯\displaystyle\Sigma_{1}\text{-}{\sf C}_{\omega-1/\omega}<_{\sf tW}\cdots<𝗍𝖶Σ1​-​𝖢n/n+1<𝗍𝖶Σ1​-​𝖢n−1/n<𝗍𝖶⋯<𝗍𝖶Σ1​-​𝖢1/2<𝗍𝖶⋯\displaystyle<_{\sf tW}\Sigma_{1}\text{-}{\sf C}_{n/n+1}<_{\sf tW}\Sigma_{1}\text{-}{\sf C}_{n-1/n}<_{\sf tW}\cdots<_{\sf tW}\Sigma_{1}\text{-}{\sf C}_{1/2}<_{\sf tW}\cdots⋯\displaystyle\cdots<𝗍𝖶⋯<𝗍𝖶Σ1​-​𝖢1/n<𝗍𝖶Σ1​-​𝖢1/n+1<𝗍𝖶⋯<𝗍𝖶Σ1​-​𝖢1/ω.\displaystyle<_{\sf tW}\cdots<_{\sf tW}\Sigma_{1}\text{-}{\sf C}_{1/n}<_{\sf tW}\Sigma_{1}\text{-}{\sf C}_{1/n+1}<_{\sf tW}\cdots<_{\sf tW}\Sigma_{1}\text{-}{\sf C}_{1/\omega}.

Below, we show the strictness of the hierarchy of theΣ1\Sigma_{1}-unique-choice problemsΣ1​-​𝖢1/n\Sigma_{1}\text{-}{\sf C}_{1/n}.
We postpone the separation of the hierarchy of theΣ1\Sigma_{1}-co-unique-choice problemsΣ1​-​𝖢n−1/n\Sigma_{1}\text{-}{\sf C}_{n-1/n}until Section5, where we provide a separation in a stronger form.

## Theorem 2.12.

Σ1​-​𝖢1/n<𝗍𝖶Σ1​-​𝖢1/n+1\Sigma_{1}\text{-}{\sf C}_{1/n}<_{\sf tW}\Sigma_{1}\text{-}{\sf C}_{1/n+1}.

## Proof.

AssumeΣ1​-​𝖢1/n+1≤𝗍𝖶Σ1​-​𝖢1/n\Sigma_{1}\text{-}{\sf C}_{1/n+1}\leq_{\sf tW}\Sigma_{1}\text{-}{\sf C}_{1/n}via(ν,ψ)(\nu,\psi).
By using the recursion theorem, construct the following algorithmeeinvolving a self-reference:
- (1)

Wait forψ(e,i)↓<n+1\psi(e,i){\downarrow}<n+1for alli<ni<n.
- (2)

If this happens, by the pigeonhole principle, there isk<n+1k<n+1such thatk≠ψ​(e,i)k\not=\psi(e,i)for anyi<ni<n.
In this case, declareφe​(k)↓\varphi_{e}(k){\downarrow}, whereφe\varphi_{e}does not terminate for other inputs.
In other words,Se={k}S_{e}=\{k\}.

Verification:
- •

Ase∈ω=dom+​(Σ1​-​𝖢1/n+1)e\in\omega={\rm dom}_{+}(\Sigma_{1}\text{-}{\sf C}_{1/n+1})andn=cod+​(Σ1​-​𝖢1/n)n={\rm cod}_{+}(\Sigma_{1}\text{-}{\sf C}_{1/n}), by totality ofψ:ω×n→n+1\psi\colon\omega\times n\to n+1, the computation ofψ​(e,i)\psi(e,i)terminates for anyi<ni<n.

Hence, the algorithmeemust reach at (2), soSeS_{e}becomes a singleton, which meanse∈dom​(Σ1​-​𝖢1/n+1)e\in{\rm dom}(\Sigma_{1}\text{-}{\sf C}_{1/n+1}).
Asν\nuis an inner reduction, we getν​(e)∈dom​(Σ1​-​𝖢1/n)\nu(e)\in{\rm dom}(\Sigma_{1}\text{-}{\sf C}_{1/n}); that is,Sν​(e)={i}S_{\nu(e)}=\{i\}for somei<ni<n.
- •

Asψ\psiis an outer reduction, for each solutioni∈Sν​(e)i\in S_{\nu(e)}, we must haveψ​(e,i)∈Se\psi(e,i)\in S_{e}; however, our construction ensuresψ​(e,i)≠k\psi(e,i)\not=kandSe={k}S_{e}=\{k\}, which is impossible.

## 2.2.2.Markov’s principle

Markov’s principlestates that “if it is impossible for a computation not to halt, then the computation halts,” and
this principle enables us to perform an unbounded search.
Logically, it corresponds to thedouble negation elimination forΣ1\Sigma_{1}-formulas(since the termination of a computation isΣ1\Sigma_{1}-complete).
We consider the following potted problems related Markov’s principle:

## Definition 2.13.

The problem𝖬𝖯𝖯𝖱{\sf MP}_{\sf PR}(Markov’s principle for computations) is defined as follows:
- •

dom+​(𝖬𝖯𝖯𝖱)=ω{\rm dom}_{+}({\sf MP}_{\sf PR})=\omega, andcod+​(𝖬𝖯𝖯𝖱)=ω{\rm cod}_{+}({\sf MP}_{\sf PR})=\omega.
- •

dom(𝖬𝖯𝖯𝖱)={e∈ω:φe(0)↓}{\rm dom}({\sf MP}_{\sf PR})=\{e\in\omega:\varphi_{e}(0)\downarrow\}.
- •

𝖬𝖯𝖯𝖱(e)={s∈ω:φe(0)[s]↓}{\sf MP}_{\sf PR}(e)=\{s\in\omega:\varphi_{e}(0)[s]\downarrow\}

## Definition 2.14.

The problem𝖬𝖯{\sf MP}(Markov’s principle for functions) is defined as follows:
- •

dom+​(𝖬𝖯)={e∈ω:φe​is total and two-valued}{\rm dom}_{+}({\sf MP})=\{e\in\omega:\varphi_{e}\mbox{ is total and two-valued}\}, andcod+​(𝖬𝖯)=ω{\rm cod}_{+}({\sf MP})=\omega.
- •

dom​(𝖬𝖯)={e∈dom+​(𝖬𝖯):∃n.φe​(n)=1}{\rm dom}({\sf MP})=\{e\in{\rm dom}_{+}({\sf MP}):\exists n.\ \varphi_{e}(n)=1\}.
- •

𝖬𝖯​(e)={n∈ω:φe​(n)=1}{\sf MP}(e)=\{n\in\omega:\varphi_{e}(n)=1\}

## Proposition 2.15.

𝖬𝖯≡𝗍𝖶𝖬𝖯𝖯𝖱{\sf MP}\equiv_{\sf tW}{\sf MP}_{\sf PR}.

## Proof.

(≥𝗍𝖶\geq_{\sf tW})
Define total reductionsν,ψ\nu,\psias follows:
- •

Inner reduction.Each indexe∈ωe\in\omegais transformed into the following primitive recursive function:φν​(e)​(n)={1if​φe​(0)​[n]↓0otherwise.\varphi_{\nu(e)}(n)=\begin{cases}1&\mbox{ if }\varphi_{e}(0)[n]\downarrow\\
0&\mbox{ otherwise.}\end{cases}

Asφν​(e)\varphi_{\nu(e)}is total and two-valued, we haveν​(e)∈dom+​(𝖬𝖯)\nu(e)\in{\rm dom}_{+}({\sf MP}).
In particular,ν:dom+​(𝖬𝖯𝖯𝖱)→dom+​(𝖬𝖯)\nu\colon{\rm dom}_{+}({\sf MP}_{\sf PR})\to{\rm dom}_{+}({\sf MP})is total.
- •

Outer reduction.For eache,s∈ωe,s\in\omega, putψ​(e,s)=s\psi(e,s)=s.
Clearly, this is total.

Verification:
- •

Inner reduction.For each instancee∈dom+​(𝖬𝖯𝖯𝖱)e\in{\rm dom}_{+}({\sf MP}_{\sf PR}), we haveφe​(0)↓\varphi_{e}(0){\downarrow}, which meansφe​(0)​[s]↓\varphi_{e}(0)[s]{\downarrow}for somess; thusφν​(e)​(s)=1\varphi_{\nu(e)}(s)=1.
Therefore,ν​(e)∈dom+​(𝖬𝖯)\nu(e)\in{\rm dom}_{+}({\sf MP}).
- •

Outer reduction.For each solutions∈𝖬𝖯​(ν​(e))s\in{\sf MP}(\nu(e)), we haveφν​(e)​(s)=1\varphi_{\nu(e)}(s)=1; in particular,φe​(0)​[s]↓\varphi_{e}(0)[s]{\downarrow}.
Hence, we gets∈𝖬𝖯𝖯𝖱​(e)s\in{\sf MP}_{\sf PR}(e).

(≤𝗍𝖶\leq_{\sf tW})
Define total reductionsν,ψ\nu,\psias follows:
- •

Inner reduction.For eache∈dom​(𝖬𝖯)e\in\mathrm{dom}(\sf MP), it is an index of a total function.
Consider the following algorithmν​(e)\nu(e):φν​(e)​(0)\varphi_{\nu(e)}(0)searches fornnsuch thatφe​(n)=1\varphi_{e}(n)=1, and then outputs the least suchnn.
This always gives some algorithmν​(e)\nu(e), soν\nuis total.
- •

Outer reduction.For eache,se,s, simulate the computation ofφν​(e)​(0)\varphi_{\nu(e)}(0)up to stagess; if the computation has halted, letψ​(e,s)\psi(e,s)be the output value, otherwise putψ​(e,s)=0\psi(e,s)=0.
Then,ψ\psiis total.

Verification.
- •

Inner reduction.For each instancee∈dom​(𝖬𝖯)e\in{\rm dom}({\sf MP}), we haveφe​(n)=1\varphi_{e}(n)=1for somenn.
Then, by our construction,φν​(e)​(0)\varphi_{\nu(e)}(0)eventually terminates the computation, which meansν​(e)∈dom​(𝖬𝖯𝖯𝖱)\nu(e)\in{\rm dom}({\sf MP}_{\sf PR}).
- •

Outer reduction.For each solutions∈𝖬𝖯𝖯𝖱​(ν​(e))s\in{\sf MP}_{\sf PR}(\nu(e)),φν​(e)​(0)\varphi_{\nu(e)}(0)terminates the computation by stagess; hence, one can find annnsuch thatφe​(n)=1\varphi_{e}(n)=1by that stage, and thusψ​(e,s)=n\psi(e,s)=n.
Therefore,ψ​(e,s)∈𝖬𝖯​(e)\psi(e,s)\in{\sf MP}(e).

∎

In our context, Markov’s principle corresponds to theΣ1\Sigma_{1}-choice problem (which is equivalent to theΣ1\Sigma_{1}-unique-choice problem by Proposition2.10) onω\omega.
Thus, Markov’s principle is the hardest problem in the hierarchy ofΣ1\Sigma_{1}-choice problems (⋆\star‣2.2.1)

## Proposition 2.16.

𝖬𝖯≡𝗍𝖶Σ1​-​𝖢1/ω{\sf MP}\equiv_{\sf tW}\Sigma_{1}\text{-}{\sf C}_{1/\omega}.

## Proof.

By Propositions2.10and2.15, it suffices to show𝖬𝖯𝖯𝖱≤𝗍𝖶Σ1​-​𝖢≥1/ω≤𝗍𝖶𝖬𝖯{\sf MP}_{\sf PR}\leq_{\sf tW}\Sigma_{1}\text{-}{\sf C}_{\geq 1/\omega}\leq_{\sf tW}{\sf MP}.

𝖬𝖯𝖯𝖱≤𝗍𝖶Σ1​-​𝖢≥1/ω{\sf MP}_{\sf PR}\leq_{\sf tW}\Sigma_{1}\text{-}{\sf C}_{\geq 1/\omega}:
- •

Inner reduction.Givene∈ωe\in\omega, letν​(e)\nu(e)be an index of theΣ1\Sigma_{1}set{s∈ω:φe(0)[s]↓}\{s\in\omega:\varphi_{e}(0)[s]\downarrow\}.
Thenν\nuis total.
- •

Outer reduction.For eache,se,s, putψ​(e,s)=s\psi(e,s)=s.
Clearly, this is total.

Obviously,(ν,ψ)(\nu,\psi)gives the actual reduction.

Σ1​-​𝖢≥1/ω≤𝗍𝖶𝖬𝖯\Sigma_{1}\text{-}{\sf C}_{\geq 1/\omega}\leq_{\sf tW}{\sf MP}:
- •

Inner reduction.Givene∈ωe\in\omega, defineφν​(e)\varphi_{\nu(e)}as follows:φν​(e)​(⟨n,s⟩)={1if​φe​(n)​[s]↓0otherwise.\varphi_{\nu(e)}(\langle n,s\rangle)=\begin{cases}1&\mbox{ if }\varphi_{e}(n)[s]{\downarrow}\\
0&\mbox{ otherwise.}\end{cases}

Asφν​(e)\varphi_{\nu(e)}is total and two-valued, we haveν​(e)∈dom+​(𝖬𝖯)\nu(e)\in{\rm dom}_{+}({\sf MP}).
In particular,ν:dom+​(Σ1​-​𝖢≥1/ω)→dom+​(𝖬𝖯)\nu\colon{\rm dom}_{+}(\Sigma_{1}\text{-}{\sf C}_{\geq 1/\omega})\to{\rm dom}_{+}({\sf MP})is total.
- •

Outer reduction.Putψ​(e,⟨n,s⟩)=n\psi(e,\langle{n,s}\rangle)=n. Thenψ\psiis total.

Verification:
- •

Inner reduction.For each instancee∈dom​(Σ1​-​𝖢≥1/ω)e\in{\rm dom}(\Sigma_{1}\text{-}{\sf C}_{\geq 1/\omega}), we haveSe={n∈ω:φe(n)↓}≠∅S_{e}=\{n\in\omega:\varphi_{e}(n){\downarrow}\}\not=\emptyset, which meansφe​(n)↓\varphi_{e}(n){\downarrow}for somenn; in particular,φe​(n)​[s]↓\varphi_{e}(n)[s]{\downarrow}for somess.
Thus, we getν​(e)∈dom​(𝖬𝖯)\nu(e)\in{\rm dom}({\sf MP}).
- •

Outer reduction.For each solution⟨n,s⟩∈𝖬𝖯​(ν​(e))\langle{n,s}\rangle\in{\sf MP}(\nu(e)), we haveφν​(e)​(⟨n,s⟩)=1\varphi_{\nu(e)}(\langle{n,s}\rangle)=1.
By our construction, this meansφe​(n)↓\varphi_{e}(n){\downarrow}; hence,ψ​(e,⟨n,s⟩)=n∈Se\psi(e,\langle{n,s}\rangle)=n\in S_{e}.

∎

A weakened version of Markov’s principle is known asdisjunctive Markov’s principle, which corresponds to theDe Morgan’s law forΠ1\Pi_{1}formulas; see e.g.[13].
There is a hierarchy𝖬𝖯n∨{\sf MP}_{n}^{\lor}of disjunctive Markov’s principle in constructive mathematics.

## Definition 2.17.

For eachn≤ωn\leq\omega,𝖬𝖯n∨{\sf MP}_{n}^{\lor}is defined as follows:
- •

dom+(𝖬𝖯n∨)={e∈ω:∀k<n∀t∈ω.φe(k,t)↓<2}{\rm dom}_{+}({\sf MP}_{n}^{\lor})=\{e\in\omega:\forall k<n\forall t\in\omega.\ \varphi_{e}(k,t){\downarrow}<2\}, andcod+​(𝖬𝖯n∨)=n{\rm cod}_{+}({\sf MP}_{n}^{\lor})=n.
- •

dom​(𝖬𝖯n∨)={e∈dom+​(𝖬𝖯n∨):∃!⁡k<n​∃t.φe​(k,t)=1}{\rm dom}({\sf MP}_{n}^{\lor})=\{e\in{\rm dom}_{+}({\sf MP}_{n}^{\lor}):\exists!k<n\exists t.\ \varphi_{e}(k,t)=1\}.
- •

𝖬𝖯n∨​(e)={k<n:∀t.φe​(k,t)=0}{\sf MP}_{n}^{\lor}(e)=\{k<n:\forall t.\ \varphi_{e}(k,t)=0\}

In our context, disjunctive Markov’s principle corresponds to theΣ1\Sigma_{1}-co-unique choice problem (which is equivalent to theΠ1\Pi_{1}-co-unique choice problem and theΣ1\Sigma_{1}-all-or-co-unique choice problem by Theorem2.11).

## Theorem 2.18.

𝖬𝖯n∨≡𝗍𝖶Σ1​-​𝖢n−1/n{\sf MP}_{n}^{\lor}\equiv_{\sf tW}\Sigma_{1}\text{-}{\sf C}_{n-1/n}for anyn≤ωn\leq\omega.

## Proof.

By Theorem2.11, it suffices to show𝖬𝖯n∨≡𝗍𝖶Π1​-​𝖢n−1/n{\sf MP}_{n}^{\lor}\equiv_{\sf tW}\Pi_{1}\text{-}{\sf C}_{n-1/n}, but this is obvious.
∎

## 2.2.3.Lessor Limited Principle of Omniscience LLPO

Some ofΠ1\Pi_{1}-choice problems are computably unsolvable.
In classical computability theory, a vast amount of research has been conducted on this topic.
In the context of Weihrauch reducibility,Π1​-​𝖢≥k/n\Pi_{1}\text{-}{\sf C}_{\geq k/n}is studied under the name𝖠𝖢𝖢n{\sf ACC}_{n}, which stands for “all-or-counique choice”.
Here, it is linked to Richman’s𝖫𝖫𝖯𝖮n{\sf LLPO}_{n}hierarchy[33].

## Definition 2.19.

For eachn≤ωn\leq\omega,𝖫𝖫𝖯𝖮n{\sf LLPO}_{n}is defined as follows:
- •

dom+(𝖫𝖫𝖯𝖮n)={e∈ω:∀i<n∀k∈ω.φe(i,k)↓<2}{\rm dom}_{+}({\sf LLPO}_{n})=\{e\in\omega:\forall i<n\forall k\in\omega.\ \varphi_{e}(i,k){\downarrow}<2\}, andcod+​(𝖫𝖫𝖯𝖮n∨)=n{\rm cod}_{+}({\sf LLPO}_{n}^{\lor})=n.
- •

dom​(𝖫𝖫𝖯𝖮n)={e∈dom+​(𝖫𝖫𝖯𝖮n):∃t.φe​(k,t)=1​for at most onek<n}{\rm dom}({\sf LLPO}_{n})=\{e\in{\rm dom}_{+}({\sf LLPO}_{n}):\exists t.\ \varphi_{e}(k,t)=1\mbox{ for at most one $k<n$}\}.
- •

𝖫𝖫𝖯𝖮n​(e)={k<n:∀t.φe​(t)=0}{\sf LLPO}_{n}(e)=\{k<n:\forall t.\ \varphi_{e}(t)=0\}

Here, we define𝖫𝖫𝖯𝖮=𝖫𝖫𝖯𝖮2{\sf LLPO}={\sf LLPO}_{2}.

## Proposition 2.20.

𝖫𝖫𝖯𝖮n≡𝗍𝖶Π1​-​𝖢≥n−1/n{\sf LLPO}_{n}\equiv_{\sf tW}\Pi_{1}\text{-}{\sf C}_{\geq n-1/n}for anyn≤ωn\leq\omega.

## Proof.

Obvious.
∎

## Corollary 2.21.

𝖬𝖯n∨≤𝗍𝖶𝖫𝖫𝖯𝖮n{\sf MP}_{n}^{\lor}\leq_{\sf tW}{\sf LLPO}_{n}.

## Corollary 2.22.

𝖫𝖫𝖯𝖮≡𝗍𝖶Π1​-​𝖢≥1/2{\sf LLPO}\equiv_{\sf tW}\Pi_{1}\text{-}{\sf C}_{\geq 1/2}.

An important property of𝖫𝖫𝖯𝖮n{\sf LLPO}_{n}is that it is totalizable in the following sense:

dom​(Ln)=dom+​(Ln)=cod+​(Ln)=ω{\rm dom}(L_{n})={\rm dom}_{+}(L_{n})={\rm cod}_{+}(L_{n})=\omega.

## 2.3.Join of problems

The Weihrauch degrees form a lattice, and in particular, the join (the least upper bound) of two problems is given by the sum operation[6].

## Definition 2.24.

LetF:⊆X⇉YF\colon\!\!\subseteq X\rightrightarrows YandG:⊆Z⇉WG\colon\!\!\subseteq Z\rightrightarrows Wbe potted problems.
Then define the sumF+G:⊆X+Z⇉Y+WF+G\colon\!\!\subseteq X+Z\rightrightarrows Y+Was the following potted problem:
- •

dom+​(F+G)=X+Y={⟨0,x⟩:x∈X}∪{⟨1,y⟩:y∈Y}{\rm dom}_{+}(F+G)=X+Y=\{\langle{0,x}\rangle:x\in X\}\cup\{\langle{1,y}\rangle:y\in Y\}.
- •

cod+​(F+G)=Z+W{\rm cod}_{+}(F+G)=Z+W.
- •

dom​(F+G)=dom​(F)+dom​(G){\rm dom}(F+G)={\rm dom}(F)+{\rm dom}(G).
- •

(F+G)​(0,x)=⟨0,F​(x)⟩(F+G)(0,x)=\langle{0,F(x)}\rangleand(F+G)​(1,y)=⟨1,G​(y)⟩(F+G)(1,y)=\langle{1,G(y)}\rangle.

Note that(F+G)​(i,x)(F+G)(i,x)is of the form⟨i,z⟩\langle{i,z}\rangle, but since this notation can often be cumbersome, this is sometimes simply written aszz.

## Proposition 2.25.

LetF,GF,Gbe potted problems.
ThenF+GF+Gis the least upper bound ofFFandGGwith respect to≤𝗍𝖶\leq_{\sf tW}.

## Proof.

Upper bound:LetFi:⊆Xi⇉YiF_{i}\colon\!\!\subseteq X_{i}\rightrightarrows Y_{i}be given for eachi<2i<2.
We showFi≤𝗍𝖶F0+F1F_{i}\leq_{\sf tW}F_{0}+F_{1}.
- •

Inner reduction.Defineιi:Xi→X0+X1\iota_{i}\colon X_{i}\to X_{0}+X_{1}asιi​(x)=(i,x)\iota_{i}(x)=(i,x).
Clearly, this is total.
- •

Outer reduction.Defineψi:Xi×(Y0+Y1)→Yi\psi_{i}\colon X_{i}\times(Y_{0}+Y_{1})\to Y_{i}asψi​(x,⟨i,y⟩)=y\psi_{i}(x,\langle{i,y}\rangle)=yandψi​(x,⟨1−i,y⟩)=0\psi_{i}(x,\langle{1-i,y}\rangle)=0.
Here,y∈Yiy\in Y_{i}, and recall that0is always contained in the potential domain; in particular,0∈Y1−i0\in Y_{1-i}.
Hence,ψi\psi_{i}is total.

Verification:
- •

Inner reduction.For each instancex∈dom​(Fi)x\in{\rm dom}(F_{i}), we haveιi​(x)=(i,x)∈dom​(F0+F1)\iota_{i}(x)=(i,x)\in{\rm dom}(F_{0}+F_{1}).
- •

Outer reduction.For each solution⟨k,y⟩∈(F0+F1)​(ιi​(x))=⟨i,Fi​(x)⟩\langle{k,y}\rangle\in(F_{0}+F_{1})(\iota_{i}(x))=\langle{i,F_{i}(x)}\rangle, we clearly haveψi​(x,⟨k,y⟩)=y∈Fi​(x)\psi_{i}(x,\langle{k,y}\rangle)=y\in F_{i}(x).

Least:LetG:⊆Z⇉WG\colon\!\!\subseteq Z\rightrightarrows Wbe a potted problem such thatF0,F1≤𝗍𝖶GF_{0},F_{1}\leq_{\sf tW}G, whereFi≤𝗍𝖶GF_{i}\leq_{\sf tW}Gis witnessed by total reductions(νi,ψi)(\nu_{i},\psi_{i}).
We showF0+F1≤𝗍𝖶GF_{0}+F_{1}\leq_{\sf tW}G.

Inner reduction.Defineν:X0+X1→Z\nu\colon X_{0}+X_{1}\to Zasν​(i,x)=νi​(x)\nu(i,x)=\nu_{i}(x).
Since eachνi:Xi→Z\nu_{i}\colon X_{i}\to Zis total, so isν\nu.

Outer reduction.Defineψ:(X0+X1)×W→Y0+Y1\psi\colon(X_{0}+X_{1})\times W\to Y_{0}+Y_{1}asψ​(⟨i,x⟩,y)=⟨i,ψi​(x,y)⟩\psi(\langle{i,x}\rangle,y)=\langle{i,\psi_{i}(x,y)}\rangle.

One can also introduce other Weihrauch operations such as the meet operation for potted problems; however, we will omit them in this article since we do not use them.

## 3.Many-Query Reducibility

## 3.1.Oracle-as-polynomial

In computability theory, there are various notion of reducibility; among them,Turing reducibility—the most fundamental—allows formultiple queriesto an oracle.
In contrast, Weihrauch reducibility≤𝖶\leq_{\sf W}is a notion ofsingle-queryreducibility.
An extension of this notion to multi-query reducibility≤𝖶𝖦\leq_{\sf W}^{\sf G}is provided via what is known as areduction game[15].
Here, we introduce the multi-query version of total Weihrauch reducibility≤𝗍𝖶\leq_{\sf tW}.
However, since its definition appears quite complicated at first glance, we will first explain the simple abstract idea behind it; see also Ahman-Bauer[2,3]and Pradic-Price[31,32].
- (1)

Aone-querycomputation using an oracle capable of solving a problemF:X⇉YF\colon{X}\rightrightarrows Yis an element of the followingpolynomial:∑x∈XVF​(x):={(x,ψ)∣x∈X​and​ψ:F​(x)→V​is computable}.\sum_{x\in{X}}V^{F(x)}:=\{(x,\psi)\mid x\in{X}\mbox{ and }\psi\colon{F(x)}\to V\mbox{ is computable}\}.

An element(x,ψ)(x,\psi)of this polynomial is the following process:
Make a queryx∈Xx\in Xto the oracleFFonce, receive a responsey∈F​(x)y\in F(x), and then output a valueψ​(y)\psi(y)of typeVV.
- (2)

Aone-query reductionfrom a problemFFtoGGis a transformation from “one-query computations usingFFas an oracle” into “one-query computations usingGGas an oracle”; that is, a morphism between polynomialsλ​V.∑x∈XVF​(x)→λ​V.∑z∈ZVG​(z)\lambda V.\sum_{x\in X}V^{F(x)}\to\lambda V.\sum_{z\in Z}V^{G(z)}.
By a simple calculation (abstractly speaking, Yoneda’s Lemma), this can be identified with an element of the following set:∏x∈X∑z∈ZF(x)G​(z):={(ν,ψ)computable∣\displaystyle\prod_{x\in X}\sum_{z\in Z}F(x)^{G(z)}:=\{(\nu,\psi)\mbox{ computable}\mid∀x(x∈X→ν(x)∈Zand\displaystyle\forall x(x\in X\to\nu(x)\in Z\mbox{ and }∀y(y∈G(ν(x))→ψ(x,y)∈F(x)))}.\displaystyle\forall y(y\in G(\nu(x))\to\psi(x,y)\in F(x)))\}.

Each element(ν,ψ)(\nu,\psi)is nothing other than a Weihrauch reduction.
- (3)

Amulti-query oracle computationusingFFcan be described by composing the polynomial functiorλ​V.∑x∈XVF​(x)\lambda V.\sum_{x\in X}V^{F(x)}the required number of times.
To be precise, since the number of queries during a computation is unknown in advance, a multi-query oracle computation is given as the least fixed point of iterated composition, which is known as the so-calledfree monadover the above polynomial functor:μ​S.(∑x∈XSF​(x)+V).\mu S.\left(\sum_{x\in X}S^{F(x)}\;+\;V\right).

Here,μ\muis the least fixed-point operator.
This is the following process:
- (a)

TheComputerfirst selects whether to move to the left or right of the++sign.
That choice is encoded asi∈{0,1}i\in\{0,1\}.
- (b)

i=1i=1means that the current state has moved to the right.
Then, terminates the computation, and output a value of typeVV.
- (c)

i=0i=0means that the current state has moved to the left.
Then, make a queryx∈Xx\in Xto the oracleFF, receive a responsey∈F​(x)y\in F(x), and then reach at the stateSS.

Here,SSis a fixed point, into which the entire expression inside the parentheses is substituted.
This means that the state returns to the initial position (a) again, and go to the next round of the computation.

A multi-query computation (3) can be regarded as an interactive game betweenOracleFF(namedMerlinbelow) andComputer(namedArthurbelow).
This is the key idea behind a reduction game.
With this in mind, we provide a game description of the free monadμ​S.(∑x∈XSF​(x)+V)\mu S.\left(\sum_{x\in X}S^{F(x)}\;+\;V\right)in the potted setting.

## 3.2.Total Weihrauch game

## Definition 3.1.

LetU¯\underline{U}be a potted set, andVVbe a set.
For a potted problemFF, consider the following two player perfect information game𝔊𝗍​(U¯;F;V)\mathfrak{G}^{\sf t}(\underline{U};F;V):𝙼𝚎𝚛𝚕𝚒𝚗:x0x1…xk𝙰𝚛𝚝𝚑𝚞𝚛:y0y1…yk\begin{array}[]{rlllllllll}{\tt Merlin}\colon&x_{0}&&x_{1}&&\dots&x_{k}&\\
{\tt Arthur}\colon&&y_{0}&&y_{1}&\dots&&y_{k}\end{array}

Rule(potential):
Thep.rulerequires two players to comply with the following:
- •

Merlinplaysx0∈U+x_{0}\in U_{+}in round0, andxn∈cod+​(F)x_{n}\in{\rm cod}_{+}(F)in subsequent roundsn>0n>0.
- •

Arthurplaysyn=⟨i,zn⟩∈dom+​(F)+Vy_{n}=\langle{i,z_{n}}\rangle\in{\rm dom}_{+}(F)+Vin roundnn.
- –

i=0i=0is a declaration to proceed to the next round of the game.
- –

i=1i=1is a declaration to terminate the game.
- •

## Observation 3.3.

≤𝗍𝖶𝖦\leq_{\sf tW}^{\sf G}is preorder.

## Proof.

Although the idea behind the proof is trivial, we postpone the detailed exposition until Proposition4.19.
Abstractly speaking: A multi-query reduction is a natural transformation between free monads (regarded as polynomial functors), so the transitivity follows from the composition of natural transformations.
∎

## Remark(Multi-query reducibility for decision problems).

On the one hand, the notion ofTuring reducibilityis recovered as multi-query reducibility≤𝖶𝖦\leq_{\sf W}^{\sf G}restricted to single-valued total functionsω→2\omega\to 2.
On the other hand, the notion oftruth-table reducibilityis recovered as multi-query total reducibility≤𝗍𝖶𝖦\leq_{\sf tW}^{\sf G}restricted to single-valued total functionsω→2\omega\to 2.

## 3.3.Example

In Theorem2.12, we showed that the≤𝗍𝖶\leq_{\sf tW}-strength ofΣ1​-​𝖢1/n\Sigma_{1}\text{-}{\sf C}_{1/n}varies for different values ofnn.
However, under the more powerful reducibility≤𝗍𝖶𝖦\leq_{\sf tW}^{\sf G}, which allows many queries, the hierarchy ofΣ1​-​𝖢1/n\Sigma_{1}\text{-}{\sf C}_{1/n}collapses.

Σ1​-​𝖢1/2≡𝗍𝖶𝖦Σ1​-​𝖢1/n\Sigma_{1}\text{-}{\sf C}_{1/2}\equiv_{\sf tW}^{\sf G}\Sigma_{1}\text{-}{\sf C}_{1/n}for anyn<ωn<\omega.

ThenArthur’s next query isb​(e,ℓ)b(e,\ell).

Note thatSb​(e,ℓ)⊆nS_{b(e,\ell)}\subseteq n.

Totality.Regardless ofe,ℓ,ke,\ell,k, the queriesa​(e)a(e),b​(e,ℓ)b(e,\ell)and the outputℓ​n+k\ell n+kare always defined.
Hence, the above yields a total strategy.

Therefore, there is a uniqueℓ\ellsuch thatSe,ℓS_{e,\ell}is a singleton, and in this case, we haveSa​(e)={ℓ}S_{a(e)}=\{\ell\}.

## 3.4.Computation Tree

Games are intuitively easy to understand, but they are not well-suited for rigorous arguments.
By focusing on trees of strategies (game trees), we can use terminology on trees, making it easier to describe the formal details of the discussion.
It is easier to describe the formal details of a discussion by using terminology on trees, via a game tree (strategy trees).
The translation betweenterminating gamesandlabeled well-founded treesis well-known in various contexts; e.g. a translation between the least fixed point (the term algebra) and its syntax tree.
Those familiar with type theory might find it helpful to think of a W-type (a type of well-founded trees / the initial algebra of a polynomial functor).
We describe game trees in the potted setting.

## Terminology(on trees).
- •

Atreeis a set of finite strings closed under initial segments.
Then the empty string is also called aroot.
A maximal string is called aleaf.
- •

A tree iswell-foundedif it has no infinite path.
- •

We use the symbolT×{}^{\times}\!Tto denote the set of all leaves of a treeTT.
We also putT∘=T∖T×{}^{\circ}\!T=T\setminus{}^{\times}\!T.
Eachσ∈T∘\sigma\in{}^{\circ}\!Tis called aninternal node.
- •

We say that a treeT⊆Y<ωT\subseteq Y^{<\omega}isfull-branchingif for any internal nodeσ∈T∘\sigma\in{}^{\circ}\!Tandi∈Yi\in Y, we haveσ​⌢​i∈T\sigma\mbox{}^{\smallfrown}i\in T.

## Remark.

IfT⊆Y<ωT\subseteq Y^{<\omega}is a full-branching well-founded tree, then for anyα∈Yω\alpha\in Y^{\omega}there isnnsuch thatα↾n∈T×\alpha\upharpoonright n\in{}^{\times}\!T.

## Construction.

LetF:⊆X⇉YF\colon\!\!\subseteq X\rightrightarrows Ybe a potted problem.
Analyze the game𝔊𝗍​(U¯;F;V)\mathfrak{G}^{\sf t}(\underline{U};F;V)in Definition4.11.

Strategy⇒\RightarrowComputation Tree:Arthur’s strategy induces a labeled well-founded tree.
- •

The set ofMerlin’s possible plays form a well-founded tree.
- •

Arthur’s moves are recorded as labels on the internal nodes.

Recall thatArthur’s strategy is a total computable functionη:U+×Y<ω→X+V.\eta\colon U_{+}\times Y^{<\omega}\to X+V.

Potential:
For eachp.inputn∈U+n\in U_{+}, thep.computation treeinduced byη\etais the following labeled well-founded tree(Υn,νn,ψn)(\Upsilon_{n},\nu_{n},\psi_{n}):
- •

Υn⊆Y<ω\Upsilon_{n}\subseteq Y^{<\omega}is a computable full-branching well-founded tree.
- –

σ∈Υn\sigma\in\Upsilon_{n}is a leaf iffσ\sigmais a minimal string satisfyingη​(n,σ)=(1,y)\eta(n,\sigma)=(1,y)for someyy.
- –

Hence,Υn\Upsilon_{n}is the tree of all plays ofMerlinobeying thep.rule.
- •

At each internal node ofΥn\Upsilon_{n}, the strategyη\etamakes a query inXX.
Formally, define the query-labeling functionνn:Υn∘→X\nu_{n}\colon{}^{\circ}\!\Upsilon_{n}\to Xas follows:
- –

The above item onΥn\Upsilon_{n}ensures that, for eachσ∈Υn∘\sigma\in{}^{\circ}\!\Upsilon_{n}, we haveη​(n,σ)=(0,x)\eta(n,\sigma)=(0,x)for somex∈Xx\in X; hence, we putνn​(σ)=x\nu_{n}(\sigma)=x.
- •

At each leaf ofΥn\Upsilon_{n}, the strategy outputs a value of typeVV.
Formally, define the output-labeling functionψn:Υn×→V\psi_{n}\colon{}^{\times}\!\Upsilon_{n}\to Vas follows:
- –

The above item onΥn\Upsilon_{n}ensures that, for eachσ∈Υn×\sigma\in{}^{\times}\!\Upsilon_{n}, we haveη​(n,σ)=(1,y)\eta(n,\sigma)=(1,y)for somey∈Vy\in V; hence, we putψn​(σ)=y\psi_{n}(\sigma)=y.

Actual:
For eacha.inputn∈U−n\in U_{-}, thea.computation treeforΦηF​(n)\Phi^{F}_{\eta}(n)induced byη\etais the following “FF-branching” subtree𝕋n⊆Υn\mathbb{T}_{n}\subseteq\Upsilon_{n}:

𝕋n⊆Υn\mathbb{T}_{n}\subseteq\Upsilon_{n}has a root.
For each internal nodeσ∈Υn∘\sigma\in{}^{\circ}\!\Upsilon_{n}, if we have already putσ∈𝕋n\sigma\in\mathbb{T}_{n}andνn​(σ)∈dom​(F)\nu_{n}(\sigma)\in{\rm dom}(F)then we putσ​⌢​i∈𝕋n\sigma\mbox{}^{\smallfrown}i\in\mathbb{T}_{n}for anyi∈F​(νn​(σ))i\in F(\nu_{n}(\sigma)).
- •

In other words,𝕋n\mathbb{T}_{n}is the tree of all plays byMerlinobeying thea.rule.
- •

The label function is given by restricting the aboveψn\psi_{n}andνn\nu_{n}to𝕋n\mathbb{T}_{n}.

## Observation 3.5.

There is an effective one-to-one correspondence betweenp.strategies for𝔊𝗍​(U¯;F;V)\mathfrak{G}^{\sf t}(\underline{U};F;V)and computable functionsn∈U+↦(Υn,νn,ψn)n\in U_{+}\mapsto(\Upsilon_{n},\nu_{n},\psi_{n})satisfying the following:

To align with the notation in traditional computability theory, one may writeψσ​(n)\psi^{\sigma}(n)orψ​(σ;n)\psi(\sigma;n)instead ofψn​(σ)\psi_{n}(\sigma).

A treeΥ\Upsiloncan be recovered from(ν,ψ)(\nu,\psi):

Thus, the pair(ν,ψ)(\nu,\psi)control the whole computation.
A code of the computation(ν,ψ)(\nu,\psi)is a pair of indices of partial computable functions:⊆ω<ω→ω\colon\!\!\subseteq\omega^{<\omega}\to\omegamaximally extendingψ,ν\psi,\nu.
A computation of codeeeis written as(νe,ψe)(\nu^{e},\psi^{e}).

## 3.5.Computability

In the context of total reducibility, a (partial) computable oracle also play an important role.
First, we analyze the conditions under which an oracle generates only computable functions.

## Definition 3.7.

LetF:⊆X⇉YF\colon\!\!\subseteq X\rightrightarrows Ybe a problem.
- •

We say thatG:⊆X⇉YG\colon\!\!\subseteq X\rightrightarrows YrefinesF:⊆X⇉YF\colon\!\!\subseteq X\rightrightarrows Yifdom​(F)⊆dom​(G){\rm dom}(F)\subseteq{\rm dom}(G)andG​(x)⊆F​(x)G(x)\subseteq F(x)for anyx∈dom​(F)x\in{\rm dom}(F).
- •

We say thatf:⊆X→Yf\colon\!\!\subseteq X\to Yis achoice functionforF:⊆X⇉YF\colon\!\!\subseteq X\rightrightarrows Yifdom​(F)⊆dom​(f){\rm dom}(F)\subseteq{\rm dom}(f)andf​(x)∈F​(x)f(x)\in F(x)for anyx∈dom​(F)x\in{\rm dom}(F).
- •

We say thatFFis(total) computableif it has a (total) computable choice function.

Below,idω:ω→ω{\rm id}_{\omega}\colon\omega\to\omegais the total identity function onω\omega.

## Proposition 3.8.

For a potted problemF:⊆X⇉YF\colon\!\!\subseteq X\rightrightarrows Y:F≤𝗍𝖶𝖦idω⇔Fis total computable.F\leq_{\sf tW}^{\sf G}{\rm id}_{\omega}\iff\mbox{$F$ is total computable.}

## Proof.

(⇒\Rightarrow)Sketch:
- •

Since the solution to a queryqqtoidω{\rm id}_{\omega}isqqitself,𝙼𝚎𝚛𝚕𝚒𝚗{\tt Merlin}’s play can be simulated in a computable manner.
Therefore, by feeding𝙼𝚎𝚛𝚕𝚒𝚗{\tt Merlin}’s computable play into𝙰𝚛𝚝𝚑𝚞𝚛{\tt Arthur}’s total computable strategy, we can compute the outputf​(n)f(n)for each inputn∈dom+​(F)n\in{\rm dom}_{+}(F).
Thisffis a total computable choice function forFF.

Details.The precise construction offfis given as follows:
- •

For eachp.inputn∈dom+​(F)n\in{\rm dom}_{+}(F), one can compute ap.tree(Υn,νn,ψn)(\Upsilon_{n},\nu_{n},\psi_{n}).
- •

Givenn∈dom+​(F)n\in{\rm dom}_{+}(F), we inductively compute a leafρ​(n)∈Υn×\rho(n)\in{}^{\times}\!\Upsilon_{n}as follows:
- –

Letρ0\rho_{0}be the root ofΥn\Upsilon_{n}, i.e., the empty string.
We inductively assume that we have already computed a nodeρs∈Υn\rho_{s}\in\Upsilon_{n}of lengthss
- –

Ifρs\rho_{s}is an internal node ofΥn\Upsilon_{n}, the total computable functionνn\nu_{n}makes a queryνn​(ρs)\nu_{n}(\rho_{s})to the oracleidω{\rm id}_{\omega}.
Since its solution is the query itself, putρs+1=ρs​⌢​νn​(ρs)\rho_{s+1}=\rho_{s}\mbox{}^{\smallfrown}\nu_{n}(\rho_{s}).
- –

Ifρs\rho_{s}is a leaf ofΥn\Upsilon_{n}, putρ​(n)=ρs\rho(n)=\rho_{s}.
- •

Forn∈dom+​(F)n\in{\rm dom}_{+}(F), definef​(n)=ψn​(ρ​(n))f(n)=\psi_{n}(\rho(n)).
Thenffis a total computable function.

Verification:
- •

For eacha.inputn∈dom​(F)n\in{\rm dom}(F), this construction give a unique leafρ​(n)∈𝕋n×\rho(n)\in{}^{\times}\!\mathbb{T}_{n}in thea.computation tree𝕋n⊆Υn\mathbb{T}_{n}\subseteq\Upsilon_{n}.
Since(Υn,νn,ψn)(\Upsilon_{n},\nu_{n},\psi_{n})solvesF​(n)F(n), we getf​(n)=ψn​(ρ​(n))∈F​(n)f(n)=\psi_{n}(\rho(n))\in F(n).

Consequently,ffis a total computable choice forFF.
This shows thatFFis total computable.

(⇐\Leftarrow)
Assume thatFFis total computable.
Then, there is a total computable choicef:X→Yf\colon X\to YforFF.
Note thatffrefinesFF; in particular,F≤𝗍𝖶fF\leq_{\sf tW}fholds.
We showf≤𝗍𝖶idωf\leq_{\sf tW}{\rm id}_{\omega}.
- •

Inner reduction.For eachn∈Xn\in X, putν​(n)=n\nu(n)=n; thenν\nuis clearly total.
- •

Outer reduction.For eachn∈Xn\in Xandm∈ωm\in\omega, putψ​(n,m)=f​(n)\psi(n,m)=f(n).
Asffis total computable onXX, so isψ\psi.

This clearly gives a reductionf≤𝗍𝖶idωf\leq_{\sf tW}{\rm id}_{\omega}.
Thus, we getF≤𝗍𝖶f≤𝗍𝖶idωF\leq_{\sf tW}f\leq_{\sf tW}{\rm id}_{\omega}.
∎

Recall from Proposition2.16that Markov’s principle𝖬𝖯{\sf MP}corresponds toΣ1\Sigma_{1}-unique choiceΣ1​-​𝖢1/ω\Sigma_{1}\text{-}{\sf C}_{1/\omega}.

## Theorem 3.9.

For a potted problemF:⊆X⇉YF\colon\!\!\subseteq X\rightrightarrows Y:F≤𝗍𝖶𝖦Σ1​-​𝖢1/ω⇔Fis computable,F\leq_{\sf tW}^{\sf G}\Sigma_{1}\text{-}{\sf C}_{1/\omega}\iff\mbox{$F$ is computable,}

## Proof.

(⇒\Rightarrow)Sketch.
- •

Note that𝖬𝖯{\sf MP}has a (partial) computable choicehh.
Ifn∈dom​(F)n\in{\rm dom}(F), any query made by𝙰𝚛𝚝𝚑𝚞𝚛{\tt Arthur}must be indom​(𝖬𝖯){\rm dom}({\sf MP}), so the computation ofhhto find its solution always terminates.
This means that𝙼𝚎𝚛𝚕𝚒𝚗{\tt Merlin}’s play can be simulated in a partial computable manner.
Therefore, by feeding𝙼𝚎𝚛𝚕𝚒𝚗{\tt Merlin}’s partial computable play into𝙰𝚛𝚝𝚑𝚞𝚛{\tt Arthur}’s total computable strategy, we can compute an outputf​(n)f(n)for eacha.inputn∈dom​(F)n\in{\rm dom}(F).
Thisffis a computable choice function forFF.

Details.PutM=𝖬𝖯𝖯𝖱M={\sf MP}_{\sf PR}, and assumeF≤𝗍𝖶𝖦MF\leq_{\sf tW}^{\sf G}Mviaee.
We construct a choiceΓe​(n)\Gamma_{e}(n)forF​(n)F(n).
- •

For eachp.inputn∈dom+​(F)n\in{\rm dom}_{+}(F), the indexeeyields ap.computation tree(Υne,νne,ψne)(\Upsilon^{e}_{n},\nu^{e}_{n},\psi^{e}_{n}).
Hereafter, the superscripteewill be omitted.
- •

Givena.inputn∈dom​(F)n\in{\rm dom}(F), one can compute thea.computation tree𝕋n⊆Υn\mathbb{T}_{n}\subseteq\Upsilon_{n}and its leftmost leafρ​(n)∈𝕋n×\rho(n)\in{}^{\times}\!\mathbb{T}_{n}.
In details, we inductively compute the leafρ​(n)∈Υn×\rho(n)\in{}^{\times}\!\Upsilon_{n}as follows:
- –

Letρ0\rho_{0}be the root of𝕋n\mathbb{T}_{n}.
We inductively assume that we have already computed a nodeρs∈𝕋n\rho_{s}\in\mathbb{T}_{n}of lengthss.
- –

Ifρs\rho_{s}is an internal node of𝕋n\mathbb{T}_{n}, the total computable functionνn\nu_{n}makes a queryνn​(ρs)=q\nu_{n}(\rho_{s})=qtoMM.

## Remark.

Here, althoughδ\deltais total,Γe=φδ​(e)\Gamma_{e}=\varphi_{\delta(e)}itself is a partial function.

Recall from Corollary2.22that𝖫𝖫𝖯𝖮{\sf LLPO}corresponds toΠ1​-​𝖢≥1/2\Pi_{1}\text{-}{\sf C}_{\geq 1/2}.
The following is a reformulation of a basic observation in classical computability theory.
In realizability theory, this forms the core of Lifschitz realizability.

## Theorem 3.10.

For a single-valued problemf:⊆ω→ωf\colon\!\!\subseteq\omega\to\omega:f≤𝗍𝖶𝖦Σ1​-​𝖢1/ω+Π1​-​𝖢≥1/2⇔fis computable.f\leq_{\sf tW}^{\sf G}\Sigma_{1}\text{-}{\sf C}_{1/\omega}+\Pi_{1}\text{-}{\sf C}_{\geq 1/2}\iff\mbox{$f$ is computable.}

## Proof.

(⇐\Leftarrow)
By Theorem3.9, ifffis computable, then we havef≤𝗍𝖶𝖦Σ1​-​𝖢1/ωf\leq_{\sf tW}^{\sf G}\Sigma_{1}\text{-}{\sf C}_{1/\omega}; in particular, we getf≤𝗍𝖶𝖦Σ1​-​𝖢1/ω+Π1​-​𝖢≥1/2f\leq_{\sf tW}^{\sf G}\Sigma_{1}\text{-}{\sf C}_{1/\omega}+\Pi_{1}\text{-}{\sf C}_{\geq 1/2}.

(⇒\Rightarrow)
Assumef≤𝗍𝖶𝖦Gf\leq_{\sf tW}^{\sf G}Gviaee.
For eachp.inputn∈dom+​(f)n\in{\rm dom}_{+}(f), the indexeeyields ap.computation tree(Υne,νne,ψne)(\Upsilon^{e}_{n},\nu^{e}_{n},\psi^{e}_{n}).
Hereafter, the superscripteewill be omitted.

Idea.
- •

For anyn∈dom​(f)n\in{\rm dom}(f), thea.computation tree𝕋n\mathbb{T}_{n}is a well-founded binary tree; hence, it is a finite tree by (the contrapositive of) weak König’s lemma.
- •

Moreover, this finite tree𝕋n\mathbb{T}_{n}is aΠ1\Pi_{1}tree (where theΠ1\Pi_{1}-ness is caused byΠ1​-​𝖢≥1/2\Pi_{1}\text{-}{\sf C}_{\geq 1/2}), so its computable upper approximation𝕋n​[t]\mathbb{T}_{n}[t]converges to𝕋n\mathbb{T}_{n}at some finite stage.
- •

By single-valuedness off​(n)f(n),ψn\psi_{n}is constant on the leaves of𝕋n\mathbb{T}_{n}.
By finiteness of𝕋n\mathbb{T}_{n}, we eventually observe thatψn\psi_{n}is constant on the leaves of𝕋n​[t]\mathbb{T}_{n}[t]at some stagett.
After observing this, we output the unique value ofψn\psi_{n}, which must bef​(n)f(n).

Details.We construct an algorithmΓe​(n)\Gamma_{e}(n)to computef​(n)f(n)as follows:
- (1)

At each stagett, proceed as follows:
Declare that the root of the treeΥn\Upsilon_{n}is active.
Letσ∈Υn∘\sigma\in{}^{\circ}\!\Upsilon_{n}be an internal node.
Inductively assume thatσ\sigmais active.
- (2)

Ifνn​(σ)\nu_{n}(\sigma)makes a queryqqtoΣ1​-​𝖢1/ω\Sigma_{1}\text{-}{\sf C}_{1/\omega},
check whether some element is enumerated into theqqthΣ1\Sigma_{1}setSq⊆ωS_{q}\subseteq\omegaby stagett.
- •

If some element is enumerated, for the first suchk∈Sqk\in S_{q}, we declare thatσ​⌢​k\sigma\mbox{}^{\smallfrown}kis active, while the nodeσ\sigmais switched to inactive.
- •

If no element is enumerated, keepσ\sigmaactive but in the wait state.
- (3)

Ifνn​(σ)\nu_{n}(\sigma)makes a queryqqtoΠ1​-​𝖢2\Pi_{1}\text{-}{\sf C}_{2}, look at the approximationPq​[t]P_{q}[t]of theqqthΠ1\Pi_{1}setPq⊆{0,1}P_{q}\subseteq\{0,1\}at stagett.
- •

If we currently havei∈Pq​[t]i\in P_{q}[t], declare thatσ​⌢​i\sigma\mbox{}^{\smallfrown}iis active.
- •

If we recognizei∉Pq​[t]i\not\in P_{q}[t], all nodes extendingσ​⌢​i\sigma\mbox{}^{\smallfrown}iare switched to inactive.

If we recognizePq​[t]=∅P_{q}[t]=\emptyset, keepσ\sigmaactive but in the wait state.

If there is no such a node, go to (5).
- (5)

Verification:
- •

For eacha.inputn∈dom​(f)n\in{\rm dom}(f), thea.computation tree𝕋n⊆Υn\mathbb{T}_{n}\subseteq\Upsilon_{n}has exactly one branching at aΣ1​-​𝖢1/ω\Sigma_{1}\text{-}{\sf C}_{1/\omega}-querying node, and at most two branching atΠ1​-​𝖢≥1/2\Pi_{1}\text{-}{\sf C}_{\geq 1/2}-querying node; in particular, finitely branching.

Before all leaves of𝕋n\mathbb{T}_{n}become active, there is always an active node in the wait state.
Furthermore, every leaf eventually becomes active after some stage.
This is because:
- –

If an internal nodeσ∈𝕋n∘\sigma\in{}^{\circ}\!\mathbb{T}_{n}makes a queryqqtoΣ1​-​𝖢1/ω\Sigma_{1}\text{-}{\sf C}_{1/\omega}, then at stagett, eitherσ\sigmain the wait state for something to be enumerated intoSqS_{q}, or else the unique successor ofσ\sigmain𝕋n\mathbb{T}_{n}is set to active.

- •

## 4.Extended Oracle

## 4.1.Total Extended Weihrauch reducibility

When dealing with pure logical principles (with no complexity bounds), some previous studies have employed a notion called “extended predicates” or “bilayer functions” (see Bauer[4], Kihara[24], and Kihara-Ng[20]).
In Kihara’s context, this is a function involvingpublicandsecretinputs, and computability is involved only on the public inputs.

## Definition 4.1.

Apotted bilayer problemis a tuple(X,Λ,Y;F)(X,\Lambda,Y;F)consisting of setsX,Y⊆ωX,Y\subseteq\omegacontaining0, an (arbitrary) setΛ\Lambdaand a subsetF⊆X×Λ×YF\subseteq X\times\Lambda\times Y.
We use the following notations and terminologies:
- •

We writeF:⊆X×Λ⇉YF\colon\!\!\subseteq X\times\Lambda\rightrightarrows Yfor such a tuple.
- •

The productX×ΛX\times\Lambdais denoted bydom+​(F){\rm dom}_{+}(F), which s called thepotential domain(or thep.domain).
- •

YYis denoted bycod+​(F){\rm cod}_{+}(F), which is called thepotential codomain(or thep.codomain).
- •

For each(x,α)∈X×Λ(x,\alpha)\in X\times\Lambda,F​(x|α)F(x|\alpha)is defined as{y∈Y:(x,α,y)∈F}\{y\in Y:(x,\alpha,y)\in F\}.
- •

dom​(F){\rm dom}(F)is defined as{(x,α)∈X×Λ:F​(x|α)≠∅}\{(x,\alpha)\in X\times\Lambda:F(x|\alpha)\not=\emptyset\}, which is called theactual domain(or thea.domain).
- •

IfF​(x|α)F(x|\alpha)is a singleton{y}\{y\}, then we writeF​(x|α)=yF(x|\alpha)=y.
- •

IfF​(x|α)F(x|\alpha)is a singleton for each(x|α)∈dom​(F)(x|\alpha)\in{\rm dom}(F), then we say thatFFissingle-valuedand writeF:⊆X×Λ→YF\colon\!\!\subseteq X\times\Lambda\to Y.

For each(x|α)∈X×Λ(x|\alpha)\in X\times\Lambda,xxis called apublicinstance, andα\alphais called asecretinstance.
A usual problem can be thought of as a function that has only public instances.

## Remark.

Each potted problemF:⊆X⇉YF\colon\!\!\subseteq X\rightrightarrows Yis always identified with the following potted bilayer problemF~:⊆X×{∗}⇉Y\tilde{F}\colon\!\!\subseteq X\times\{\ast\}\rightrightarrows Y:F~​(x|∗)=F​(x).\tilde{F}(x|\ast)=F(x).

Hereinafter,F~\tilde{F}will also be written simply asFF.

The notion ofextended Weihrauch reducibilitywas first introduced by Bauer[4].
Then Kihara[24]described it as a reduction between bilayer problems involving secret data:
A reduction between bilayer problems consists of machinesψ\psiandν\nu, which can only see the public input, and an advisorζ\zeta, which sees both the public and secret inputs and makes a secret query.

## Definition 4.2.

LetF:⊆X×Λ⇉YF\colon\!\!\subseteq X\times\Lambda\rightrightarrows YandG:⊆Z×Δ⇉WG\colon\!\!\subseteq Z\times\Delta\rightrightarrows Wbe potted bilayer problems.
We say thatFFistotal extended Weihrauch reducible toGGif there are partial computable functionsν,ψ\nu,\psiand a functionζ\zeta(not necessarily computable) such that the following holds:
- (1)

ν:X→Z\nu\colon X\to Zis total.
- (2)

ζ:X×Λ→Δ\zeta\colon X\times\Lambda\to\Deltais total.
- (3)

ψ:X×W→Y\psi\colon X\times W\to Yis total.
- (4)

(x|α)∈dom​(F)(x|\alpha)\in{\rm dom}(F)implies(x~|α~):=(ν(x)|ζ(x|α))∈dom(G)(\tilde{x}|\tilde{\alpha}):=(\nu(x)|\zeta(x|\alpha))\in{\rm dom}(G).
- (5)

(x|α)∈dom​(F)(x|\alpha)\in{\rm dom}(F)andy∈G​(x~|α~)y\in G(\tilde{x}|\tilde{\alpha})implyψ​(x,y)∈F​(x|α)\psi(x,y)\in F(x|\alpha).

In this case, we writeF≤𝗍𝖶GF\leq_{\sf tW}G.
We callν\nuapublic inner reduction,ζ\zetaasecret inner reductionandψ\psianouter reduction.

The reader might think it would be more appropriate to useteWrather thantWfor the subscript, but in this article we have avoided making the notation complicated.
In fact, the reduction itself remains the same; only the type of problems under consideration changes.
That is, a reduction is still a morphism of polynomial functors; the only change is that the objects have shifted from “monosemically-coded” sets (modest sets) to “polysemically-coded” sets (assemblies).
Henceforth, we simply refer to the notion of reducibility described above as total Weihrauch reducibility.

## 4.2.Examples (One-Query Reducibility)

Since the definition looks quite complicated, let us look at a few simple concrete examples before analyzing this relation≤𝗍𝖶\leq_{\sf tW}.

## Definition 4.3.

For0<k<n≤ω0<k<n\leq\omega, the choice principle𝖢k/n{\sf C}_{k/n}is defined as follows:
- •

dom+​(𝖢k/n)={∗}×𝒫​(n){\rm dom}_{+}({\sf C}_{k/n})=\{\ast\}\times\mathcal{P}(n).
- •

dom​(𝖢k/n)={∗}×{A⊆n:|A|=k}{\rm dom}({\sf C}_{k/n})=\{\ast\}\times\{A\subseteq n:|A|=k\}.
- •

cod+​(𝖢k/n)=n{\rm cod}_{+}({\sf C}_{k/n})=n.
- •

𝖢k/n(∗|A)=A{\sf C}_{k/n}(\ast|A)=A; that is, anya∈Aa\in Ais a solution to𝖢k/n(∗|A){\sf C}_{k/n}(\ast|A).

## Definition 4.4.

For0<k<n≤ω0<k<n\leq\omega, the choice principle𝖢≥k/n{\sf C}_{\geq k/n}is defined as follows:
- •

dom+​(𝖢≥k/n)={∗}×𝒫​(n){\rm dom}_{+}({\sf C}_{\geq k/n})=\{\ast\}\times\mathcal{P}(n).
- •

dom​(𝖢≥k/n)={∗}×{A⊆n:|A|≥k}{\rm dom}({\sf C}_{\geq k/n})=\{\ast\}\times\{A\subseteq n:|A|\geq k\}.
- •

cod+​(𝖢≥k/n)=n{\rm cod}_{+}({\sf C}_{\geq k/n})=n.
- •

𝖢≥k/n(∗|A)=A{\sf C}_{\geq k/n}(\ast|A)=A; that is, anya∈Aa\in Ais a solution to𝖢≥k/n(∗|A){\sf C}_{\geq k/n}(\ast|A).

The unique choice problem𝖢1/n{\sf C}_{1/n}can be identified with the following “secret” identity function:
- •

dom+​(𝖢1/n)=dom​(𝖢1/n)={∗}×n{\rm dom}_{+}({\sf C}_{1/n})={\rm dom}({\sf C}_{1/n})=\{\ast\}\times n.
- •

cod+​(𝖢1/n)=n{\rm cod}_{+}({\sf C}_{1/n})=n.
- •

𝖢1/n(∗|a)=a{\sf C}_{1/n}(\ast|a)=a; that isaais a solution to𝖢1/n(∗|a){\sf C}_{1/n}(\ast|a).

## Remark.

The choice problem𝖢1/2{\sf C}_{1/2}is associated with theweak law of excluded middle¬A∨¬¬A\neg A\lor\neg\neg A, which is equivalent to𝖢≥1/2{\sf C}_{\geq 1/2}realizingde Morgan’s law¬(A∧B)→¬A∨¬B\neg(A\land B)\to\neg A\lor\neg B.
In general, the all-or-co-unique choice problem𝖢≥n−1/n{\sf C}_{\geq n-1/n}can be associated with a variant of the weak law of excluded middle𝐖𝐋𝐄𝐌¯k\underline{{\bf WLEM}}_{k}(see Section7.3).
Similarly, the choice problem𝖢≥1/ω{\sf C}_{\geq 1/\omega}may be linked to de Morgan’s law𝐃𝐌𝐋¯ℕ\underline{{\bf DML}}_{\mathbb{N}}on the natural numbers¬∀xℕ​P​(x)→∃xℕ​¬P​(x)\neg\forall x^{\mathbb{N}}P(x)\to\exists x^{\mathbb{N}}\neg P(x)(see Section7.3).

## Proposition 4.5.

𝖢1/n≡𝗍𝖶𝖢≥1/n{\sf C}_{1/n}\equiv_{\sf tW}{\sf C}_{\geq 1/n}for anyn≤ωn\leq\omega.

## Proof.

(≤𝗍𝖶\leq_{\sf tW})
This is obvious since the left-hand side is the restriction of the right-hand side.

(≥𝗍𝖶\geq_{\sf tW})
Total public reductionsν,ψ\nu,\psiand a total secret reductionζ\zetaare given as follows:
- •

Inner reduction.For public putν​(∗)=∗\nu(\ast)=\ast.
This is clearly total computable.
For secret, putζ(∗|∅)=∅\zeta(\ast|\emptyset)=\emptysetandζ(∗|A)=minA\zeta(\ast|A)=\min AforA≠∅A\not=\emptyset.
This is clearly total.
- •

Outer reduction.Putψ​(∗,k)=k\psi(\ast,k)=k.
This is clearly total computable.

Verification:
- •

Inner reduction.For each instance(∗|A)∈dom(𝖢≥1/n)(\ast|A)\in{\rm dom}({\sf C}_{\geq 1/n}), we haveA≠∅A\not=\emptyset, which is mapped to(∗|minA)∈dom(𝖴𝖢k)(\ast|\min A)\in{\rm dom}({\sf UC}_{k}).
- •

Outer reduction.For each solutionk=𝖢1/n(∗|minA)k={\sf C}_{1/n}(\ast|\min A), we getψ(∗,k)=k=minA∈A=𝖢≥1/n(∗|A)\psi(\ast,k)=k=\min A\in A={\sf C}_{\geq 1/n}(\ast|A).

∎

## Proposition 4.6.

𝖢n−1/n≡𝗍𝖶𝖢≥n−1/n{\sf C}_{n-1/n}\equiv_{\sf tW}{\sf C}_{\geq n-1/n}for anyn≥ωn\geq\omega.

## Proof.

(≤𝗍𝖶\leq_{\sf tW})
This is obvious since the left-hand side is the restriction of the right-hand side.

(≥𝗍𝖶\geq_{\sf tW})
Total public reductionsν,ψ\nu,\psiand a secret reductionζ\zetaare given as follows:
- •

Inner reduction.For public, putν​(∗)=∗\nu(\ast)=\ast.
This is clearly total computable.
For secret, givenA⊆kA\subseteq k, the following gives a total function:ζ(∗|A)={A∖{0}if​A=nAif​A≠n\zeta(\ast|A)=\begin{cases}A\setminus\{0\}&\mbox{ if }A=n\\
A&\mbox{ if }A\not=n\end{cases}
- •

Outer reduction.Putψ​(∗,k)=k\psi(\ast,k)=k.
This is clearly total computable.

Verification:
- •

Inner reduction.For each instance(∗|A)∈dom(𝖢≥n−1/n)(\ast|A)\in{\rm dom}({\sf C}_{\geq n-1/n}), we have|k∖A|≤1|k\setminus A|\leq 1.
IfA=kA=kthenη(∗|A)=A∖{0}\eta(\ast|A)=A\setminus\{0\}is a co-singleton.
IfA≠kA\not=kthenη(∗|A)=A\eta(\ast|A)=Ais also a co-singleton.
In any case, we have(∗|ζ(∗|A))∈dom(𝖢n−1/n)(\ast|\zeta(\ast|A))\in{\rm dom}({\sf C}_{n-1/n}).
- •

Outer reduction.For each solutionk∈𝖢n−1/n(∗|ζ(∗|A))k\in{\sf C}_{n-1/n}(\ast|\zeta(\ast|A)), we getψ(∗,k)=k∈ζ(∗|A)⊆A=𝖢≥n−1/n(∗|A)\psi(\ast,k)=k\in\zeta(\ast|A)\subseteq A={\sf C}_{\geq n-1/n}(\ast|A).

∎

We show that𝖢1/ω{\sf C}_{1/\omega}is harder than any potted problem.

## Proposition 4.7.

F≤𝗍𝖶𝖢1/ωF\leq_{\sf tW}{\sf C}_{1/\omega}for any potted problemF:⊆X⇉YF\colon\!\!\subseteq X\rightrightarrows Y.

## Proof.

By Proposition4.5, it suffices to showF≤𝗍𝖶𝖢≥1/ωF\leq_{\sf tW}{\sf C}_{\geq 1/\omega}.
Recall that an instancex∈Xx\in XforFFis identified with(x|∗)(x|\ast).
Total public reductionsν,ψ\nu,\psiand a secrete reductionζ\zetaare given as follows:
- •

Inner reduction.For public, putν​(x)=∗\nu(x)=\astfor anyx∈Xx\in X.
This is clearly total computable.
For secret, ifx∉dom​(F)x\not\in{\rm dom}(F)then putζ​(x|∗)=∅\zeta(x|\ast)=\emptyset, and ifx∈dom​(F)x\in{\rm dom}(F)then putζ​(x|∗)=F​(x)\zeta(x|\ast)=F(x).
This is total.
- •

Outer reduction.Putψ​(x,y)=y\psi(x,y)=y.
This is clearly total computable.

Verification:
- •

Inner reduction.For each instance(x|∗)∈dom​(F)(x|\ast)\in{\rm dom}(F), we have∅≠F​(x)⊆ω\emptyset\not=F(x)\subseteq\omega, which is mapped to(∗|F(x))∈dom(𝖢≥1/ω)(\ast|F(x))\in{\rm dom}({\sf C}_{\geq 1/\omega}).
- •

Outer reduction.For each solutiony∈𝖢≥1/ω(∗|F(x))y\in{\sf C}_{\geq 1/\omega}(\ast|F(x)), we getψ​(x,y)=y∈F​(x)\psi(x,y)=y\in F(x).

∎

As a variation of Proposition4.7, we see that𝖢1/2{\sf C}_{1/2}provides an upper bound for two-valued problems.

## Proposition 4.8.

For any potted problemF:⊆X⇉YF\colon\!\!\subseteq X\rightrightarrows Y, if|Y|≤n|Y|\leq nthenF≤𝗍𝖶𝖢1/nF\leq_{\sf tW}{\sf C}_{1/n}.

## Proof.

By Proposition4.5, it suffices to showF≤𝗍𝖶𝖢≥1/nF\leq_{\sf tW}{\sf C}_{\geq 1/n}.
Recall that an instancex∈Xx\in XforFFis identified with(x|∗)(x|\ast).
As|Y|≤n|Y|\leq n, there is a surjectionh:n→Yh\colon n\to Y.
Sincehhis a finite function, one can treat this as a computable function.
Total public reductionsν,ψ\nu,\psiand a secrete reductionζ\zetaare given as follows:
- •

Inner reduction.For public, putν​(x)=∗\nu(x)=\astfor anyx∈Xx\in X.
This is clearly total computable.
For secret, ifx∉dom​(F)x\not\in{\rm dom}(F)then putζ​(x|∗)=∅\zeta(x|\ast)=\emptyset, and ifx∈dom​(F)x\in{\rm dom}(F)then putζ​(x|∗)={i≤n:h​(i)∈F​(x)}\zeta(x|\ast)=\{i\leq n:h(i)\in F(x)\}.
This is total.
- •

Outer reduction.Putψ​(x,y)=h​(y)\psi(x,y)=h(y).
This is total computable.

Verification:
- •

Inner reduction.For each instance(x|∗)∈dom​(F)(x|\ast)\in{\rm dom}(F), we have∅≠F​(x)⊆Y\emptyset\not=F(x)\subseteq Y, which is mapped to∅≠ζ​(x|∗)⊆n\emptyset\not=\zeta(x|\ast)\subseteq n.
Hence,(∗|ζ(x|∗))∈dom(𝖢≥1/n)(\ast|\zeta(x|\ast))\in{\rm dom}({\sf C}_{\geq 1/n}).
- •

Outer reduction.For each solutiony∈𝖢≥1/n(∗|ζ(x|∗))=ζ(x|∗)y\in{\sf C}_{\geq 1/n}(\ast|\zeta(x|\ast))=\zeta(x|\ast), by our definition ofζ\zeta, we haveh​(y)∈F​(x)h(y)\in F(x).
Hence. we getψ​(x,y)=h​(y)∈F​(x)\psi(x,y)=h(y)\in F(x).

∎
