## 4.3.Basic Properties

First, we must show that≤𝗍𝖶\leq_{\sf tW}forms a preorder.
The proof may seem somewhat cumbersome, but this is due to the complexity of the definition; the argument itself is self-evident.
In fact, abstractly speaking, this follows from the fact that a (total) Weihrauch reduction corresponds to a natural transformation between polynomial functors[31](and of course, the composition of natural transformations is a natural transformation).

## Proposition 4.9.

≤𝗍𝖶\leq_{\sf tW}is a preorder.

## Proof.

Reflexivity:Considerν​(x)=x\nu(x)=x,ζ​(x|α)=α\zeta(x|\alpha)=\alphaandψ​(x,y)=y\psi(x,y)=y.

Transitivity:LetF:⊆X0×Λ0⇉Y0F\colon\!\!\subseteq X_{0}\times\Lambda_{0}\rightrightarrows Y_{0},G:⊆X1×Λ1⇉Y1G\colon\!\!\subseteq X_{1}\times\Lambda_{1}\rightrightarrows Y_{1},H:⊆X2×Λ2⇉Y2H\colon\!\!\subseteq X_{2}\times\Lambda_{2}\rightrightarrows Y_{2}be potted bilayer problems such thatF≤𝗍𝖶G≤𝗍𝖶HF\leq_{\sf tW}G\leq_{\sf tW}H.
AssumeF≤𝗍𝖶GF\leq_{\sf tW}Gvia(ν,ψ|ζ)(\nu,\psi|\zeta)andG≤𝗍𝖶HG\leq_{\sf tW}Hvia(ν′,ψ′|ζ′)(\nu^{\prime},\psi^{\prime}|\zeta^{\prime}); that is, we have the following functions:ν:X0→X1,\displaystyle\nu\colon X_{0}\to X_{1},ν′:X1→X2,\displaystyle\nu^{\prime}\colon X_{1}\to X_{2},ζ:X0×Λ0→Λ1,\displaystyle\zeta\colon X_{0}\times\Lambda_{0}\to\Lambda_{1},ζ′:X1×Λ1→Λ2,\displaystyle\zeta^{\prime}\colon X_{1}\times\Lambda_{1}\to\Lambda_{2},ψ:X0×X1→Y0,\displaystyle\psi\colon X_{0}\times X_{1}\to Y_{0},ψ′:X1×Y2→Y1.\displaystyle\psi^{\prime}\colon X_{1}\times Y_{2}\to Y_{1}.

We want to construct a reductionFFfromHH.
- •

Public reduction.Sinceν′′=ν′∘ν:X0→X2\nu^{\prime\prime}=\nu^{\prime}\circ\nu\colon X_{0}\to X_{2}is a total function, this satisfies the condition (1) in Definition4.2.
- •

Secret reduction.Defineζ′′\zeta^{\prime\prime}as follows:X0×Λ0\textstyle{X_{0}\times\Lambda_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}⟨ν​π0,ζ⟩\scriptstyle{\langle{\nu\pi_{0},\zeta}\rangle}X1×Λ1\textstyle{X_{1}\times\Lambda_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}ζ′\scriptstyle{\zeta^{\prime}}Λ2\textstyle{\Lambda_{2}}(x|α)\textstyle{(x|\alpha)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}(ν(x)|ζ(x|α))\textstyle{(\nu(x)|\zeta(x|\alpha))}

That is, putζ′′(x|α)=ζ′(ν(x)|ζ(x|α))\zeta^{\prime\prime}(x|\alpha)=\zeta^{\prime}(\nu(x)|\zeta(x|\alpha)), which satisfies the condition (2) in Definition4.2.
- •

Outer reduction.Defineψ′′:X0×Y2→Y0\psi^{\prime\prime}\colon X_{0}\times Y_{2}\to Y_{0}as follows:ψ′′​(x,y)=ψ​(x,ψ′​(ν​(x),y))\psi^{\prime\prime}(x,y)=\psi(x,\psi^{\prime}(\nu(x),y))

for any(x,y)∈X0×Y2(x,y)\in X_{0}\times Y_{2}.
Then, we haveν​(x)∈X1\nu(x)\in X_{1}, soy′:=ψ′​(ν​(x),y)∈Y1y^{\prime}:=\psi^{\prime}(\nu(x),y)\in Y_{1}; thus,ψ​(x,y′)∈Y0\psi(x,y^{\prime})\in Y_{0}.
Hence,ψ′′\psi^{\prime\prime}fulfills the totality condition (3) in Definition4.2.

Verification:
- •

Inner reduction.Now, we see(x|α)∈dom(F)⟹(x~|α~):=(ν(x)|ζ(x|α))∈dom(G)⟹(ν′(x~)|ζ′(x~|α~))∈dom(H).(x|\alpha)\in{\rm dom}(F)\implies(\tilde{x}|\tilde{\alpha}):=(\nu(x)|\zeta(x|\alpha))\in{\rm dom}(G)\implies(\nu^{\prime}(\tilde{x})|\zeta^{\prime}(\tilde{x}|\tilde{\alpha}))\in{\rm dom}(H).

By definition, we haveν′′​(x)=ν′​(ν​(x))=ν′​(x~)\nu^{\prime\prime}(x)=\nu^{\prime}(\nu(x))=\nu^{\prime}(\tilde{x}); thus,ζ′′(x|α)=ζ′(ν(x)|ζ(x|α))=ζ′(x~|α~)\zeta^{\prime\prime}(x|\alpha)=\zeta^{\prime}(\nu(x)|\zeta(x|\alpha))=\zeta^{\prime}(\tilde{x}|\tilde{\alpha}).
Therefore, we get(ν′′(x)|ζ′′(x|α))∈dom(H)(\nu^{\prime\prime}(x)|\zeta^{\prime\prime}(x|\alpha))\in{\rm dom}(H), which satisfies the condition (4) in Definition4.2.
- •

Outer reduction.Let(x|α)∈dom​(F)(x|\alpha)\in{\rm dom}(F)andy∈H(ν′′(x)|ζ′′(x|α))y\in H(\nu^{\prime\prime}(x)|\zeta^{\prime\prime}(x|\alpha))be given.
In particular, we have(x~|α~):=(ν(x)|ζ(x|α))∈dom(G)(\tilde{x}|\tilde{\alpha}):=(\nu(x)|\zeta(x|\alpha))\in{\rm dom}(G).
- –

SinceG≤𝗍𝖶HG\leq_{\sf tW}Hvia(ν′,ψ′)(\nu^{\prime},\psi^{\prime}), as we know(x~|α~)∈dom​(G)(\tilde{x}|\tilde{\alpha})\in{\rm dom}(G), each solutiony∈H(ν′(x~)|ζ′(x~|α~))=H(ν′′(x)|ζ′′(x|α))y\in H(\nu^{\prime}(\tilde{x})|\zeta^{\prime}(\tilde{x}|\tilde{\alpha}))=H(\nu^{\prime\prime}(x)|\zeta^{\prime\prime}(x|\alpha))yieldsψ′​(x~,y)∈G​(x~|α~)\psi^{\prime}(\tilde{x},y)\in G(\tilde{x}|\tilde{\alpha}).
- –

SinceF≤𝗍𝖶GF\leq_{\sf tW}Gvia(ν,ψ)(\nu,\psi), as we know(x|α)∈dom​(F)(x|\alpha)\in{\rm dom}(F)andψ′(x~,y)∈G(x~|α~)=G(ν(x)|ζ(x|α))\psi^{\prime}(\tilde{x},y)\in G(\tilde{x}|\tilde{\alpha})=G(\nu(x)|\zeta(x|\alpha)), we getψ′′​(x,y)=ψ​(x,ψ′​(x~,y))∈F​(x|α)\psi^{\prime\prime}(x,y)=\psi(x,\psi^{\prime}(\tilde{x},y))\in F(x|\alpha).

Therefore, the condition (5) in Definition4.2is fulfilled.
This implies that(ν′′,ψ′′)(\nu^{\prime\prime},\psi^{\prime\prime})witnessesF≤𝗍𝖶HF\leq_{\sf tW}H.

∎

The domain of a potted bilayer problem can be simplified as follows:

## Proposition 4.10.

LetF:⊆X×Λ⇉YF\colon\!\!\subseteq X\times\Lambda\rightrightarrows Ybe a potted bilayer problem.
Then the domain ofFFcan be extended to a potted setA¯=(A−;A+)\underline{A}=(A_{-};A_{+})in the following sense:
- (1)

A+=XA_{+}=X.
- (2)

There is a potted bilayer functionF^\hat{F}satisfying the following conditions:dom+​(F^)=dom+​(F)=A+×Λ,\displaystyle{\rm dom}_{+}(\hat{F})={\rm dom}_{+}(F)=A_{+}\times\Lambda,dom​(F)⊆dom​(F^)=A−×Λ.\displaystyle{\rm dom}(F)\subseteq{\rm dom}(\hat{F})=A_{-}\times\Lambda.
- (3)

F^​(x|α)=F​(x|α)\hat{F}(x|\alpha)=F(x|\alpha)for any(x|α)∈dom​(F)(x|\alpha)\in{\rm dom}(F).
- (4)

F^≡𝗍𝖶F\hat{F}\equiv_{\sf tW}F.

## Proof.

We first define a potted setA¯=(A−;A+)\underline{A}=(A_{-};A_{+}).
PutA+=XA_{+}=X, and defineA−A_{-}as:A−={x∈X:∃α∈Λ.(x|α)∈dom​(F)}.A_{-}=\{x\in X:\exists\alpha\in\Lambda.\ (x|\alpha)\in{\rm dom}(F)\}.

Definition ofF^\hat{F}:
- •

Given(x|α)∈A−×Λ(x|\alpha)\in A_{-}\times\Lambda, if(x|α)∈dom​(F)(x|\alpha)\in{\rm dom}(F)then putF^​(x|α)=F​(x|α)\hat{F}(x|\alpha)=F(x|\alpha); otherwise,F^​(x|α)=Y\hat{F}(x|\alpha)=Y.

Verification:The conditions (1),(2) and (3) in Definition4.2are clearly fulfilled.
We only show (4),
- •

≥𝗍𝖶\geq_{\sf tW}: SinceF^\hat{F}is an extension ofFF, just considerν​(x)=x\nu(x)=x,ζ​(x|α)=α\zeta(x|\alpha)=\alphaandψ​(x,y)=y\psi(x,y)=y.
- •

≤𝗍𝖶\leq_{\sf tW}:
Putν​(x)=x\nu(x)=xandψ​(x,y)=y\psi(x,y)=y.
Define the total functionζ:A−×Λ→Λ\zeta\colon A_{-}\times\Lambda\to\Lambdaas follows:
- –

Given(x|α)∈A−×Λ(x|\alpha)\in A_{-}\times\Lambda, if(x|α)∈dom​(F)(x|\alpha)\in{\rm dom}(F)then putζ​(x|α)=α\zeta(x|\alpha)=\alpha; otherwise, sincex∈A−x\in A_{-}, there isβ∈Λ\beta\in\Lambdasuch that(x|β)∈dom​(F)(x|\beta)\in{\rm dom}(F).
Choose such aβ\beta, and putζ​(x|α)=β\zeta(x|\alpha)=\beta.

Let(x|α)∈dom​(F^)(x|\alpha)\in{\rm dom}(\hat{F})be given.
- –

If(x|α)∈dom​(F)(x|\alpha)\in{\rm dom}(F)then(ν(x)|ζ(x|α))=(x|α)∈dom(F)(\nu(x)|\zeta(x|\alpha))=(x|\alpha)\in{\rm dom}(F); thus, ify∈F​(x|α)y\in F(x|\alpha)thenψ​(x,y)=y∈F​(x|α)=F^​(x|α)\psi(x,y)=y\in F(x|\alpha)=\hat{F}(x|\alpha).
- –

If(x|α)∉dom​(F)(x|\alpha)\not\in{\rm dom}(F)then(ν(x)|ζ(x|α))=(x|β)∈dom(F)(\nu(x)|\zeta(x|\alpha))=(x|\beta)\in{\rm dom}(F); thus, ify∈F​(x|β)y\in F(x|\beta)then we getψ​(x,y)=y∈F​(x|β)⊆Y=F^​(x|α)\psi(x,y)=y\in F(x|\beta)\subseteq Y=\hat{F}(x|\alpha).

This showsF^≤𝗍𝖶F\hat{F}\leq_{\sf tW}F.

Consequently, we obtainF^≡𝗍𝖶F\hat{F}\equiv_{\sf tW}F.
∎

## Notation.

An extension such as Proposition4.10is written asF^:A¯×Λ→Y\hat{F}\colon\underline{A}\times\Lambda\to Y.
Indeed, by Proposition4.10, we may always assume that a potted bilayer problem is of the formF:X¯×Λ⇉YF\colon\underline{X}\times\Lambda\rightrightarrows Y.

## 4.4.Merlin-Arthur-Nimuegame

As a multi-query reduction game involving a bilayer problem, Kihara[24]introduced an incomplete information three-player game involving secrete moves.
In abstract terms, this is also nothing more than a free monadλ​V.μ​S.(∑x∈XSF​(x)+V)\lambda V.\mu S.(\sum_{x\in X}S^{F}(x)+V), but because the underlying objects have changed from monosemically-coded sets (modest sets) to polysemically-coded sets (assemblies), the exchange of secret data is inserted.
We consider such a game in the potted context.

## Definition 4.11.

LetU¯\underline{U}be a potted set,V⊆ωV\subseteq\omegabe a set, andΞ\Xibe an (arbitrary) set.
For a potted bilayer problemF:X×Λ⇉YF\colon X\times\Lambda\rightrightarrows Y, consider the following imperfect information three-player game𝔊𝗍​(U¯|Ξ;F;V)\mathfrak{G}^{\sf t}(\underline{U}|\Xi;F;V):𝙼𝚎𝚛𝚕𝚒𝚗:(x0|α)x1…xk𝙰𝚛𝚝𝚑𝚞𝚛:y0y1…yk𝙽𝚒𝚖𝚞𝚎:β0β1…βk\begin{array}[]{rlllllllll}{\tt Merlin}\colon&(x_{0}|\alpha)&&x_{1}&&\dots&x_{k}&\\
{\tt Arthur}\colon&&y_{0}&&y_{1}&\dots&&y_{k}\\
{\tt Nimue}\colon&&\beta_{0}&&\beta_{1}&\dots&&\beta_{k}\end{array}

Rule(potential): Thep.rulerequires three players to comply with the following:
- •

Merlin’s opening move in round0is the pair of a public movex0∈U+x_{0}\in U_{+}and a secret moveα∈Ξ\alpha\in\Xi.
In subsequent roundsn>0n>0,Merlinplays a public movexn∈cod+​(F)x_{n}\in{\rm cod}_{+}(F).
- •

Arthurplays a public moveyn=⟨i,zn⟩∈X++Vy_{n}=\langle{i,z_{n}}\rangle\in X_{+}+Vin roundnn.
- –

i=0i=0is a declaration to proceed to the next round of the game.
- –

i=1i=1is a declaration to terminate the game.
- •

Nimueplays a secrete moveβn∈Λ\beta_{n}\in\Lambdain roundnn.
- •

Arthurmust declare the termination of the game in some round (such a round may depend on a play).

Rule(actual): Thea.rulerequires two players to comply with the following:
- •

Merlin’s opening move in round0is the pair of a public movex0∈U−x_{0}\in U_{-}and a secret moveα∈Ξ\alpha\in\Xi.
- •

Arthurplays a public moveyn=⟨i,zn⟩∈X−+Vy_{n}=\langle{i,z_{n}}\rangle\in X_{-}+Vin roundnn.
- –

i=0i=0is a declaration to proceed to the next round of the game.
- –

i=1i=1is a declaration to terminate the game.
In this case, we callznz_{n}the output of this play.
- •

Nimueplays a secret moveβn∈Λ\beta_{n}\in\Lambdain roundnn.
- •

In roundn+1n+1,Merlinreturns a public solutionxn+1∈F​(zn|βn)x_{n+1}\in F(z_{n}|\beta_{n})in response toArthur-Nimue’s query.

Strategy(potential):
- •

Consider total functionsη:U+×cod+​(F)<ω→X++V\displaystyle\eta\colon U_{+}\times{\rm cod}_{+}(F)^{<\omega}\to X_{+}+Vζ:U+×cod+​(F)<ω×Ξ→Λ\displaystyle\zeta\colon U_{+}\times{\rm cod}_{+}(F)^{<\omega}\times\Xi\to\Lambda

We think ofη\etaasArthur’s strategy andζ\zetaasNimue’s strategy.
Here, we require computability only forη\eta.
- •

Fix suchη\etaandζ\zeta.
Then for a sequence(x0,x1,…,xk)∈A+×Y<ω(x_{0},x_{1},\dots,x_{k})\in A_{+}\times Y^{<\omega}and a secret instanceα∈Ξ\alpha\in\Xi, defineyn\displaystyle y_{n}=η​(x0,x1,…,xn)\displaystyle=\eta(x_{0},x_{1},\dots,x_{n})βn\displaystyle\beta_{n}=ζ​(x0,x1,…,xn|α)\displaystyle=\zeta(x_{0},x_{1},\dots,x_{n}|\alpha)

for eachn≤kn\leq k.
The sequence(x0|α,y0|β0,x1,y1|β1,…,xk,yk|βk)(x_{0}|\alpha,y_{0}|\beta_{0},x_{1},y_{1}|\beta_{1},\dots,x_{k},y_{k}|\beta_{k})obtained in this way is called aplayfollowing(η|ζ)(\eta|\zeta).
- •

Such an(η|ζ)(\eta|\zeta)is apotential strategy(p.strategy) forArthur-Nimueif, for any play following(η|ζ)(\eta|\zeta), one of the following must hold:
- –

Merlinviolates thep.rule beforeArthur-Nimuedo.
- –

Arthur-Nimueobey thep.rule and declare the termination of the game in some round.
- •

Such an(η|ζ)(\eta|\zeta)is anactual strategy(a.strategy) forArthur-Nimueif, in addition, for any play following(η|ζ)(\eta|\zeta), one of the following must hold:
- –

Merlinviolates thea.rule beforeArthur-Nimuedo.
- –

Arthur-Nimueobey thea.rule and declare the termination of the game in some round.

Strategy→\toProblem:
Ana.strategyη\etaforArthur-Nimueinduces the following potted bilayer problemΦη|ζF\Phi_{\eta|\zeta}^{F}:
- •

dom+​(Φη|ζF)=U+×Ξ{\rm dom}_{+}(\Phi_{\eta|\zeta}^{F})=U_{+}\times\Xi,dom​(Φη|ζF)=U−×Ξ{\rm dom}(\Phi_{\eta|\zeta}^{F})=U_{-}\times\Xi, andcod+​(Φη|ζF)=V{\rm cod}_{+}(\Phi_{\eta|\zeta}^{F})=V.
- •

For a publica.instancex0∈U−x_{0}\in U_{-}and a secret instanceα∈Ξ\alpha\in\Xi, we declarey∈Φη|ζF​(x0|α)y\in\Phi_{\eta|\zeta}^{F}(x_{0}|\alpha)if there is a play such thatMerlinstarts with the opening move(x0|α)(x_{0}|\alpha), all ofMerlin,ArthurandNimueobey thea.rule, and the game terminates with the outputyy.

## Definition 4.12.

LetF,GF,Gbe potted bilayer problems.
LetDG,ΞG,EGD_{G},\Xi_{G},E_{G}be such thatdom+​(G)=(DG)+×ΞG{\rm dom}_{+}(G)=(D_{G})_{+}\times\Xi_{G},dom​(G)⊆(DG)−×ΞG{\rm dom}(G)\subseteq(D_{G})_{-}\times\Xi_{G}, andEG=cod+​(G)E_{G}={\rm cod}_{+}(G).
Then we say thatGGismulti-query total Weihrauch reducibletoFFif there is ana.strategy(η|ζ)(\eta|\zeta)for the game𝔊𝗍​(DG|ΞG;F;EG)\mathfrak{G}^{\sf t}(D_{G}|\Xi_{G};F;E_{G})such that the following holds:∀x∈dom​(G).Φη|ζF​(x)⊆G​(x).\forall x\in{\rm dom}(G).\ \Phi_{\eta|\zeta}^{F}(x)\subseteq G(x).

In this case, we writeF≤𝗍𝖶𝖦GF\leq_{\sf tW}^{\sf G}G.

## Remark.

As before, we often think ofArthur’s strategy as a partial computable function onω×ω<ω\omega\times\omega^{<\omega}whose domain is maximally extended.

## 4.5.Example (Multi-Query Reducibility)

𝖢n/n+1{\sf C}_{n/n+1}is the co-unique choice onn+1n+1, whileΠ1​-​𝖢≥n−1/n\Pi_{1}\text{-}{\sf C}_{\geq n-1/n}is theΠ1\Pi_{1}all-or-co-unique choice onnn.
At first glance,𝖢n/n+1{\sf C}_{n/n+1}does not appear to implyΠ1​-​𝖢≥n−1/n\Pi_{1}\text{-}{\sf C}_{\geq n-1/n}, but as Kihara[24, Proposition 4.3]pointed out, if we allow for partially computable reductions (i.e., using Markov’s principleΣ1​-​𝖢1/ω\Sigma_{1}\text{-}{\sf C}_{1/\omega}), then𝖢n/n+1{\sf C}_{n/n+1}does implyΠ1​-​𝖢≥n−1/n\Pi_{1}\text{-}{\sf C}_{\geq n-1/n}.
In logical terms,𝖶𝖫𝖤𝖬n+1+𝖬𝖯{\sf WLEM}_{n+1}+{\sf MP}implies𝖫𝖫𝖯𝖮n{\sf LLPO}_{n}.
Here, we refine this observation.

## Proposition 4.13.

Π1​-​𝖢≥n−1/n≤𝗍𝖶𝖦𝖢n/n+1+Σ1​-​𝖢n−1/n\Pi_{1}\text{-}{\sf C}_{\geq n-1/n}\leq_{\sf tW}^{\sf G}{\sf C}_{n/n+1}+\Sigma_{1}\text{-}{\sf C}_{n-1/n}.

## Proof.

By Theorem2.11and Proposition4.6, it suffices to showΠ1​-​𝖢≥n−1/n≤𝗍𝖶𝖦𝖢≥n/n+1+Π1​-​𝖢n−1/n\Pi_{1}\text{-}{\sf C}_{\geq n-1/n}\leq_{\sf tW}^{\sf G}{\sf C}_{\geq n/n+1}+\Pi_{1}\text{-}{\sf C}_{n-1/n}.
For each instancee∈dom​(Π1​-​𝖢≥n−1/n)e\in{\rm dom}(\Pi_{1}\text{-}{\sf C}_{\geq n-1/n}), there aren+1n+1possibilities.
- •

Casei<ni<n:i∉Pei\not\in P_{e}.
- •

Casenn:Pe=nP_{e}=n.
- •

Two or more of these cases cannot occur simultaneously.

Round0:𝙰𝚛𝚝𝚑𝚞𝚛{\tt Arthur}asksNimuefor help, soNimueasks𝖢≥n/n+1{\sf C}_{\geq n/n+1}which case does not occur.
- •

IfMerlin’s response isi<ni<n, then𝙰𝚛𝚝𝚑𝚞𝚛{\tt Arthur}thinks that Caseiinever happens; that is,i∈Pei\in P_{e}, so outputsii.
- •

IfMerlin’s response isnn, then𝙰𝚛𝚝𝚑𝚞𝚛{\tt Arthur}thinks that Casenndoes not occur; that is,Pe≠nP_{e}\not=n.
Then𝙰𝚛𝚝𝚑𝚞𝚛{\tt Arthur}declares to proceed to the next round.

Round11:𝙰𝚛𝚝𝚑𝚞𝚛{\tt Arthur}makes a queryeetoΠ1​-​𝖢n−1/n\Pi_{1}\text{-}{\sf C}_{n-1/n}.
That is, requireMerlinto choose an element ofPeP_{e}.
- •

IfMerlin’s response iskk, then𝙰𝚛𝚝𝚑𝚞𝚛{\tt Arthur}thinksk∈Pek\in P_{e}, so returnskk.

Verification:
- •

Regardless of the value ofee,Arthuroutputs a value, so it is a total strategy.
- •

For each instancee∈dom​(Π1​-​𝖢≥n−1/n)e\in{\rm dom}(\Pi_{1}\text{-}{\sf C}_{\geq n-1/n}), the query in round11belongs todom​(𝖢≥n/n+1){\rm dom}({\sf C}_{\geq n/n+1}), soMerlinreturns a correct solution.
- •

If the response isi<ni<n, theniibelongs toPeP_{e}.
- •

If the response isnn, then we must havePe≠nP_{e}\not=n, soPeP_{e}is a co-singleton.
Therefore, the queryeein round11is a correct query toΠ1​-​𝖢n−1/n\Pi_{1}\text{-}{\sf C}_{n-1/n}, so the responsekkmust belong toPeP_{e}.

Consequently, we obtainΠ1​-​𝖢≥n−1/n≤𝗍𝖶𝖦𝖢≥n/n+1+Π1​-​𝖢n−1/n\Pi_{1}\text{-}{\sf C}_{\geq n-1/n}\leq_{\sf tW}^{\sf G}{\sf C}_{\geq n/n+1}+\Pi_{1}\text{-}{\sf C}_{n-1/n}.
∎

In logical terms, the above shows𝖶𝖫𝖤𝖬n+1+𝖬𝖯n∨{\sf WLEM}_{n+1}+{\sf MP}_{n}^{\lor}implies𝖫𝖫𝖯𝖮n{\sf LLPO}_{n}.
Later, we will see that the use of (a weak variant of) Markov’s principle is essential (Corollary5.5).

As in Proposition3.4, the hierarchy of unique choice problems collapses.

## Proposition 4.14.

𝖢1/2≡𝗍𝖶𝖦𝖢1/n{\sf C}_{1/2}\equiv_{\sf tW}^{\sf G}{\sf C}_{1/n}for anyn<ωn<\omega.

## Proof.

We show𝖢n2≤𝗍𝖶𝖦𝖢n{\sf C}_{n^{2}}\leq_{\sf tW}^{\sf G}{\sf C}_{n}.
- •

Round0.
Assume thatMerlinfirst plays a secret moveA⊆n2A\subseteq n^{2}.
ThenArthurasksNimuefor help, soNimuemakes the queryB={ℓ<n:A∩[ℓ​n,(ℓ+1)​n−1]≠∅}B=\{\ell<n:A\cap[\ell n,(\ell+1)n-1]\not=\emptyset\}.

IfAAis a singleton, so isBB.
- •

Round11.
Letℓ<n\ell<nbe a response fromMerlin.
ThenArthurasksNimuefor help, soNimuemakes the queryCℓ={k<n:ℓ​n+k∈A∩[ℓ​n,(ℓ+1)​n−1]}C_{\ell}=\{k<n:\ell n+k\in A\cap[\ell n,(\ell+1)n-1]\}.

Ifℓ∈B\ell\in BthenCℓC_{\ell}is a singleton.
- •

Round22.
Letkkbe a response from𝙼𝚎𝚛𝚕𝚒𝚗{\tt Merlin}.
Then,Arthuruses the received informationℓ,k\ell,kto outputℓ​n+k\ell n+kand declare the termination of the game.

Verification:
- •

Totality.Regardless ofAA, the queriesBB,CℓC_{\ell}and the outputℓ​n+k\ell n+kare always defined. Hence, the above yields a total strategy.
- •

Reduction.For each instance(∗|A)∈dom(𝖢1/n2)(\ast|A)\in{\rm dom}({\sf C}_{1/n^{2}}),AAis a singleton.
ThenB≠∅B\not=\emptysetis also a singleton, soMerlin’s response in round11is someℓ∈B\ell\in B; hence,CℓC_{\ell}is a singleton.
Thus,Merlin’s response in round22is somek∈Cℓk\in C_{\ell}.
By definition, we getℓ​n+k∈A\ell n+k\in A.
Therefore,Arthur’s outputℓ​n+k\ell n+kbelongs toAA, so this is a solution to𝖢1/n2​(A){\sf C}_{1/n^{2}}(A).

Consequently, we get𝖢n2≤𝗍𝖶𝖦𝖢n{\sf C}_{n^{2}}\leq_{\sf tW}^{\sf G}{\sf C}_{n}.
By repeating this process, we obtain𝖢1/2≤𝗍𝖶𝖦𝖢1/n{\sf C}_{1/2}\leq_{\sf tW}^{\sf G}{\sf C}_{1/n}for anyn<ωn<\omega.
∎

## 4.6.Computation Tree

Once again, computation trees are better suited for precise descriptions than games.
We extend the notion of computation tree, introduced in Section3.4, to the bilayer context.
We again use the standard translation between terminating games and labeled well-founded trees.

## Construction.

LetF:⊆X×Λ⇉YF\colon\!\!\subseteq X\times\Lambda\rightrightarrows Ybe a potted bilayer problem.
Describe a game tree for𝔊𝗍​(A|Ξ;F;B)\mathfrak{G}^{\sf t}(A|\Xi;F;B)by a computation tree.

Strategy⇒\RightarrowComputation Tree:Arthur-Nimue’s strategy induces a labeled well-founded tree.
- •

The set ofMerlin’s possible plays form a well-founded tree.
- •

Arthur’s moves are recorded as labels on the internal nodes.
- •

Nimue’s move imposes a restriction on the successors of each node.

Recall thatArthur-Nimue’s strategy consists of total functions:η:U+×Y<ω→X++V,\displaystyle\eta\colon U_{+}\times Y^{<\omega}\to X_{+}+V,ζ:U+×Y<ω×Ξ→Λ.\displaystyle\zeta\colon U_{+}\times Y^{<\omega}\times\Xi\to\Lambda.

Potential:
For each publicp.inputn∈U+n\in U_{+}and secrete inputα∈Ξ\alpha\in\Xi, thep.computation treefor induced by(η|ζ)(\eta|\zeta)is the following labeled well-founded tree(Υn,νn,ψn)(\Upsilon_{n},\nu_{n},\psi_{n}):
- •

Υn⊆Y<ω\Upsilon_{n}\subseteq Y^{<\omega}is a computable full-branching well-founded tree.
- –

σ∈Υn\sigma\in\Upsilon_{n}is a leaf iffσ\sigmais a minimal string satisfyingη​(n,σ)=(1,y)\eta(n,\sigma)=(1,y)for someyy.
- –

Hence,Υn\Upsilon_{n}is the tree of all plays ofMerlinobeying thep.rule.
- •

At each internal node ofΥn\Upsilon_{n},Arthur’s strategyη\etamakes a query inXX.
Formally, define the query-labeling functionνn:Υn∘→X\nu_{n}\colon{}^{\circ}\!\Upsilon_{n}\to Xas follows:
- –

The above item onΥn\Upsilon_{n}ensures that, for eachσ∈Υn∘\sigma\in{}^{\circ}\!\Upsilon_{n}, we haveη​(n,σ)=(0,x)\eta(n,\sigma)=(0,x)for somex∈Xx\in X; hence, we putνn​(σ)=x\nu_{n}(\sigma)=x.
- •

At each leaf ofΥn\Upsilon_{n}, the strategy outputs a value of typeVV.
Formally, define the output-labeling functionψn:Υn×→V\psi_{n}\colon{}^{\times}\!\Upsilon_{n}\to Vas follows:
- –

The above item onΥn\Upsilon_{n}ensures that, for eachσ∈Υn×\sigma\in{}^{\times}\!\Upsilon_{n}, we haveη​(n,σ)=(1,y)\eta(n,\sigma)=(1,y)for somey∈Vy\in V; hence, we putψn​(σ)=y\psi_{n}(\sigma)=y.

SinceNimue’s strategy is not controlled by computation, we separately it from the computation tree.
- •

At each internal node ofΥn\Upsilon_{n},Nimue’s strategyζ\zetamakes a advice inΛ\Lambda.

Putζn|α​(σ)=ζ​(n,σ|α)\zeta_{n|\alpha}(\sigma)=\zeta(n,\sigma|\alpha).

Actual:
For each publica.inputn∈U−n\in U_{-}and a secret inputα∈Ξ\alpha\in\Xi, thea.computation treeforΦη|ζF​(n|α)\Phi^{F}_{\eta|\zeta}(n|\alpha)induced by(η|ζ)(\eta|\zeta)is the following “FF-branching” subtree𝕋n|α⊆Υn\mathbb{T}_{n|\alpha}\subseteq\Upsilon_{n}:

For each internal nodeσ∈Υn∘\sigma\in{}^{\circ}\!\Upsilon_{n}, if we have already putσ∈𝕋n|α\sigma\in\mathbb{T}_{n|\alpha}, then for anyi∈F​(νn​(σ)|ζn|α​(σ))i\in F(\nu_{n}(\sigma)|\zeta_{n|\alpha}(\sigma))(if defined), we putσ​⌢​i∈𝕋n|α\sigma\mbox{}^{\smallfrown}i\in\mathbb{T}_{n|\alpha}.

In other words,𝕋n|α\mathbb{T}_{n|\alpha}is the tree of all plays byMerlinobeying thea.rule.
- •

The label function is given by restricting the aboveψn\psi_{n},νn\nu_{n}andζn|α\zeta_{n|\alpha}to𝕋n|α\mathbb{T}_{n|\alpha}.

Therefore, we getνn′′​(γ​⌢​τ)=νq′​(τ)∈dom​(H)\nu^{\prime\prime}_{n}(\gamma\mbox{}^{\smallfrown}\tau)=\nu^{\prime}_{q}(\tau)\in{\rm dom}(H).
- •

For each leafγ\gammaof𝕋n′′\mathbb{T}^{\prime\prime}_{n}, we haveγ∈κn​(σ)\gamma\in\kappa_{n}(\sigma)for someσ∈𝕋n×\sigma\in{}^{\times}\!\mathbb{T}_{n}.
Hence,ψn′′​(γ)=ψn​(σ)∈F​(n)\psi_{n}^{\prime\prime}(\gamma)=\psi_{n}(\sigma)\in F(n).

Note also that our construction of a computation treeΓ\GammaforF≤𝗍𝖶𝖦HF\leq_{\sf tW}^{\sf G}Hfrom the computation treesΦ\PhiforF≤𝗍𝖶𝖦GF\leq_{\sf tW}^{\sf G}GandΨ\PsiforG≤𝗍𝖶𝖦HG\leq_{\sf tW}^{\sf G}His effective.
In other words, there is a total computable functiont:ω2→ωt\colon\omega^{2}\to\omegawhich, given indicesddforΦ\PhiandeeforΨ\Psi, returns an indext​(d,e)t(d,e)forΓ\Gamma.
∎

## 4.7.Partial Weihrauch reducibility

Some of the results regarding total Weihrauch reducibility can be reduced to the discussion on traditional Weihrauch reducibility.
For this reason, we introduce traditional Weihrauch reducibility[6]here.
In the traditional context, a (bilayer) problem does not have the notion ofp.domain; therefore, thep.rule in theMerlin-Arthur-Nimuegame described in Section4.4is removed.
Traditional many-query Weihrauch reducibility is often denoted by≤𝗀𝖶\leq_{\sf gW}.
In this article, we write it as≤𝖶𝖦\leq_{\sf W}^{\sf G}, where𝗀{\sf g}and𝖦{\sf G}stand for a game, as in Definition3.2.

LetF:⊆X×Λ⇉YF\colon\!\!\subseteq{X}\times\Lambda\rightrightarrows YandG:⊆D×Ξ⇉EG\colon\!\!\subseteq{D}\times\Xi\rightrightarrows Ebe bilayer functions.
Then the following two conditions are equivalent:
- (1)

GGis multi-query Weihrauch reducible toFF.
- (2)

Givenn∈Dn\in D, there is an effective procedure returning(νn,ψn)(\nu_{n},\psi_{n})such that:
- •

νn:⊆Y<ω→X\nu_{n}\colon\!\!\subseteq Y^{<\omega}\to Xis a computable function.
- •

ψn:⊆Y<ω→E\psi_{n}\colon\!\!\subseteq Y^{<\omega}\to Eis a computable function.

Furthermore, for each instance(n|α)∈dom​(G)(n|\alpha)\in{\rm dom}(G), there is an(F;νn)(F;\nu_{n})-branching tree𝕋n|α⊆Y<ω\mathbb{T}_{n|\alpha}\subseteq Y^{<\omega}such that for any leafσ∈𝕋n|α×\sigma\in{}^{\times}\!\mathbb{T}_{n|\alpha}we haveψn(σ)↓∈G(n|α)\psi_{n}(\sigma){\downarrow}\in G(n|\alpha).

Straightforward.

## Definition 4.22.

We say that a treeT⊆X<ωT\subseteq X^{<\omega}isnn-fatif each internal nodeσ∈T∘\sigma\in{}^{\circ}\!Thas at leastnnimmediate successors;
that is, there arenndistinct elementsi1,i2,…,in∈Xi_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{n}\in Xsuch thatσ​⌢​ik∈T\sigma\mbox{}^{\smallfrown}i_{k}\in Tfor eachk≤nk\leq n.

Regarding the forward direction of the following, although Kihara[24, Proposition 4.4]essentially shows the contrapositive, we provide a direct proof here because a uniform algorithm for the forward direction will be needed later.

## Theorem 4.23.

For a partial functionf:⊆ω→ωf\colon\!\!\subseteq\omega\to\omega:f≤𝗍𝖶𝖦Σ1​-​𝖢1/ω+𝖢2/3⇔fis computable.f\leq_{\sf tW}^{\sf G}\Sigma_{1}\text{-}{\sf C}_{1/\omega}+{\sf C}_{2/3}\iff\mbox{$f$ is computable.}

## Proof.

(⇐\Leftarrow)
By Theorem3.9, ifffis computable thenf≤𝗍𝖶𝖦Σ1​-​𝖢1/ωf\leq_{\sf tW}^{\sf G}\Sigma_{1}\text{-}{\sf C}_{1/\omega}; in particular,f≤𝗍𝖶𝖦Σ1​-​𝖢1/ω+𝖢2/3f\leq_{\sf tW}^{\sf G}\Sigma_{1}\text{-}{\sf C}_{1/\omega}+{\sf C}_{2/3}.

(⇒\Rightarrow)
Assumef≤𝗍𝖶𝖦Σ1​-​𝖢1/ω+𝖢2/3f\leq_{\sf tW}^{\sf G}\Sigma_{1}\text{-}{\sf C}_{1/\omega}+{\sf C}_{2/3}viaee.
By Theorem4.21, we getf≤𝖶𝖦𝖢2/3f\leq_{\sf W}^{\sf G}{\sf C}_{2/3}, and moreover, one can effectively compute an index of such a reduction fromee.
This satisfies the following:
- •

For each instancen∈dom​(f)n\in{\rm dom}(f), an indexeeyields a strategy(νne,ψne)(\nu^{e}_{n},\psi^{e}_{n}).
Hereafter, the superscripteewill be omitted.
- •

Sincea.computation tree𝕋n⊆3<ω\mathbb{T}_{n}\subseteq 3^{<\omega}is finitely-branching and well-founded, by weak König’s lemma, it is a finite tree.
In addition, it is𝖢2/3{\sf C}_{2/3}-branching; that is, a22-fat tree.
Furthermore, for each leafσ∈𝕋n×\sigma\in{}^{\times}\!\mathbb{T}_{n}, the computationψn​(σ)\psi_{n}(\sigma)halts with outputf​(n)f(n).
Therefore,ψn\psi_{n}outputs a unique value on the leaves of the22-fat finite tree𝕋n\mathbb{T}_{n}.

We construct an algorithmΘe​(n)\Theta_{e}(n)computingf​(n)f(n).
This is given by the following brute-force algorithm:
- (1)

Search for a22-fat finite treeS⊆3<ωS\subseteq 3^{<\omega}satisfying the following:ψn\psi_{n}is constant on the setS×{}^{\times}\!Sof leaves.
- (2)

If such anSSis found, this means that there isccsuch thatψn(σ)↓=c\psi_{n}(\sigma){\downarrow}=cfor anyσ∈S×\sigma\in{}^{\times}\!S.
Compute such a valuecc; then putΘe​(n)=c\Theta_{e}(n)=c.

Verification:
- •

S=𝕋nS=\mathbb{T}_{n}satisfies the condition in the item (1); hence, the algorithm eventually reaches at (2) for someSS.
Hence,Θe​(n)\Theta_{e}(n)must always be defined.
- •

Although theSSfound in (2) is not necessarily the same as𝕋n\mathbb{T}_{n}, since bothSSand𝕋n\mathbb{T}_{n}are22-fat subtrees of3<ω3^{<\omega}, they share a common leafσ∈S×∩𝕋n×\sigma\in{}^{\times}\!S\cap{}^{\times}\!\mathbb{T}_{n}.
Our definition givesΘe​(n)=ψn​(σ)\Theta_{e}(n)=\psi_{n}(\sigma), and sinceσ∈𝕋n×\sigma\in{}^{\times}\!\mathbb{T}_{n}we also haveψn​(σ)=f​(n)\psi_{n}(\sigma)=f(n).

Consequently,ffis computable by the algorithmΘe\Theta_{e}.
One can effectively find an index ofΘe\Theta_{e}fromee, so hereafter, we refer to thisΘe\Theta_{e}asφε​(e)\varphi_{\varepsilon(e)}.
∎

If we removeΣ1​-​𝖢1/ω\Sigma_{1}\text{-}{\sf C}_{1/\omega}, we can guarantee total computability.

## Theorem 4.24.

For a partial functionf:⊆ω→ωf\colon\!\!\subseteq\omega\to\omega:f≤𝗍𝖶𝖦𝖢2/3⇔fis total computable.f\leq_{\sf tW}^{\sf G}{\sf C}_{2/3}\iff\mbox{$f$ is total computable.}

Actual.For eacha.instancen∈dom​(f)n\in{\rm dom}(f), we have ana.computation tree𝕋n⊆Υn\mathbb{T}_{n}\subseteq\Upsilon_{n}such thatψn\psi_{n}outputsf​(n)f(n)on its leaves.

Search for a22-fat finite treeS⊆ΥnS\subseteq\Upsilon_{n}satisfying the following:ψn\psi_{n}is constant on the setS×{}^{\times}\!Sof leaves.

If such anSSis found, this means that there isccsuch thatψn(σ)↓=c\psi_{n}(\sigma){\downarrow}=cfor anyσ∈S×\sigma\in{}^{\times}\!S.
Compute such a valuecc; then putΘe​(n)=c\Theta_{e}(n)=c.

If the value ofψn\psi_{n}is not uniquely determined on the leaves for any22-fat finite subtreeS⊆ΥnS\subseteq\Upsilon_{n}, then putΘe​(n)=0\Theta_{e}(n)=0.

Verification:
- •

For eachp.instancen∈ωn\in\omega, sinceΥn\Upsilon_{n}is a finite tree, there are finitely manyS⊆ΥnS\subseteq\Upsilon_{n}; therefore, determining whether (2) or (3) holds can be done in finite steps.

- •

For eacha.instancen∈dom​(f)n\in{\rm dom}(f), thea.computation treeS=𝕋nS=\mathbb{T}_{n}satisfies the condition in the item (1); hence, the algorithm eventually reaches at (2) for someSS.
Hence,Θe​(n)\Theta_{e}(n)must always be defined.
- •

Although theSSfound in (2) is not necessarily the same as𝕋n\mathbb{T}_{n}, bothSSand𝕋n\mathbb{T}_{n}are22-fat subtrees of3<ω3^{<\omega}, they share a common leafσ∈S×∩𝕋n×\sigma\in{}^{\times}\!S\cap{}^{\times}\!\mathbb{T}_{n}.
Our definition givesΘe​(n)=ψn​(σ)\Theta_{e}(n)=\psi_{n}(\sigma), and sinceσ∈𝕋n×\sigma\in{}^{\times}\!\mathbb{T}_{n}we also haveψn​(σ)=f​(n)\psi_{n}(\sigma)=f(n).

Consequently,ffis computable by the algorithmΘe\Theta_{e}.

## 5.Computability (Separations)

We first show that theΣ1\Sigma_{1}-co-unique choice problemΣ1​-​𝖢ω−1/ω\Sigma_{1}\text{-}{\sf C}_{\omega-1/\omega}(which is linked to𝖬𝖯ω∨{\sf MP}_{\omega}^{\lor}by Theorem2.18) is not total computable.

## Theorem 5.1.

Σ1​-​𝖢ω−1/ω≰𝗍𝖶𝖦idω\Sigma_{1}\text{-}{\sf C}_{\omega-1/\omega}\not\leq_{\sf tW}^{\sf G}{\rm id}_{\omega}.

## Proof.

By Proposition3.8, it suffices to showΣ1​-​𝖢ω−1/ω\Sigma_{1}\text{-}{\sf C}_{\omega-1/\omega}is not total computable.
Letφk:ω→ω\varphi_{k}\colon\omega\to\omegabe a total computable function.
By using the recursion theorem, construct the following algorithmeeinvolving a self-reference:
- (1)

Wait for the computationφk​(e)\varphi_{k}(e)to halt.
- (2)

If we recognize this, enumerate all elements other thanφk​(e)\varphi_{k}(e)intoSeS_{e}.
ThenSe=ω∖{φk​(e)}S_{e}=\omega\setminus\{\varphi_{k}(e)\}.

Verification:
- •

By totality ofφk\varphi_{k}, the computationφk​(e)\varphi_{k}(e)must halt.
Hence, the algorithmeemust reach at (2), so we haveSe=ω∖{φk​(e)}S_{e}=\omega\setminus\{\varphi_{k}(e)\}, which meanse∈dom​(Σ1​-​𝖢ω−1/ω)e\in{\rm dom}(\Sigma_{1}\text{-}{\sf C}_{\omega-1/\omega}).
However, we haveφk​(e)∉Se=Σ1​-​𝖢ω−1/ω​(e)\varphi_{k}(e)\not\in S_{e}=\Sigma_{1}\text{-}{\sf C}_{\omega-1/\omega}(e); hence,φk\varphi_{k}cannot be a total extension ofΣ1​-​𝖢ω−1/ω\Sigma_{1}\text{-}{\sf C}_{\omega-1/\omega}.

Consequently, we getΣ1​-​𝖢ω−1/ω≰𝗍𝖶𝖦idω\Sigma_{1}\text{-}{\sf C}_{\omega-1/\omega}\not\leq_{\sf tW}^{\sf G}{\rm id}_{\omega}.
∎

We next show thatΣ1​-​𝖢1/ω\Sigma_{1}\text{-}{\sf C}_{1/\omega}(linked to Markov’s principle𝖬𝖯{\sf MP}) is not derivable from𝖢1/2{\sf C}_{1/2}(linked to the weak law of excluded middle𝖶𝖫𝖤𝖬{\sf WLEM}).

## Theorem 5.2.

Σ1​-​𝖢1/ω≰𝗍𝖶𝖦𝖢1/2\Sigma_{1}\text{-}{\sf C}_{1/\omega}\not\leq_{\sf tW}^{\sf G}{\sf C}_{1/2}.
In particular,Σ1​-​𝖢1/ω≰𝗍𝖶𝖦Π1​-​𝖢1/2+Σ1​-​𝖢1/2\Sigma_{1}\text{-}{\sf C}_{1/\omega}\not\leq_{\sf tW}^{\sf G}\Pi_{1}\text{-}{\sf C}_{1/2}+\Sigma_{1}\text{-}{\sf C}_{1/2}.

We use the following lemma, which is proven by a standard compactness argument.

## Lemma 5.3.

Assume that the codomain of a potted bilayer problem𝒰\mathcal{U}is finite.
Then, for any potted problemF:⊆ω⇉ωF\colon\!\!\subseteq\omega\rightrightarrows\omega, ifF≤𝗍𝖶𝖦𝒰F\leq_{\sf tW}^{\sf G}\mathcal{U}thenFFhas a solution in the range bounded by a total computable functiongg; in other words,dom+​(F)⊆dom​(g);x∈dom​(F)⟹∃y≤g​(x).y∈F​(x).{\rm dom}_{+}(F)\subseteq{\rm dom}(g);\qquad x\in{\rm dom}(F)\implies\exists y\leq g(x).\ y\in F(x).

## Proof.

Assume that𝒰\mathcal{U}always takes values less thankk, and itsp.codomain isHHwithk⊆H⊆ωk\subseteq H\subseteq\omega.
In this case,h:=H∩kh:=H\cap kis a finite set.
Assume thatF≤𝗍𝖶𝖦𝒰F\leq_{\sf tW}^{\sf G}\mathcal{U}viaee.
- •

For eachp.instancen∈dom+​(F)n\in{\rm dom}_{+}(F), the indexeeyields ap.computation tree(Υne,νne,ψne)(\Upsilon^{e}_{n},\nu^{e}_{n},\psi^{e}_{n}).
Hereafter, the superscripteewill be omitted.
- •

SinceHn:=Υn∩h<ωH_{n}:=\Upsilon_{n}\cap h^{<\omega}is a finitely-branching well-founded tree, by weak König’s lemma, it is a finite tree.
Since the indexeespecifies a computation procedure forn↦Υnn\mapsto\Upsilon_{n}, andhhis a fixed finite set,n↦Hnn\mapsto H_{n}is also computable.
- •

At each leafσ∈Hn×\sigma\in{}^{\times}\!H_{n}, the valueψn​(σ)\psi_{n}(\sigma)is defined.
By finiteness ofHnH_{n}, the set{ψn​(σ):σ∈Hn}\{\psi_{n}(\sigma):\sigma\in H_{n}\}is also finite.

Then, consider the following algorithmΞe\Xi_{e}:
- (1)

For eachp.instancen∈dom+​(F)n\in{\rm dom}_{+}(F), computes the finite treeHnH_{n}.
Wait for seeingψn​(σ)↓\psi_{n}(\sigma){\downarrow}for all leavesσ∈Hn×\sigma\in{}^{\times}\!H_{n}.
- (2)

After observing the termination ofψn\psi_{n}on all leaves, we get the finite setRn={ψn​(σ):σ∈Hn×}R_{n}=\{\psi_{n}(\sigma):\sigma\in{}^{\times}\!H_{n}\}.
LetΞe​(n)\Xi_{e}(n)be the maximum valuemax⁡Rn\max R_{n}.

Verification:
- •

For eachp.instancen∈dom+​(F)n\in{\rm dom}_{+}(F),ψn\psi_{n}is total onΥn×{}^{\times}\!\Upsilon_{n}; in particular, for eachσ∈Hn×⊆Υn×\sigma\in{}^{\times}\!H_{n}\subseteq{}^{\times}\!\Upsilon_{n}, the computationψn​(σ)\psi_{n}(\sigma)halts at stagesσs_{\sigma}.
SinceHnH_{n}is finite, at the maximum stages=max⁡{sσ:σ∈Hn}s=\max\{s_{\sigma}:\sigma\in H_{n}\}, we recognize the termination ofψn\psi_{n}on all leaves; hence, the algorithm proceeds to (2).
Therefore,Ξe​(n)\Xi_{e}(n)eventually outputs some value.
- •

For eacha.instancen∈dom​(F)n\in{\rm dom}(F), thea.computation tree𝕋n\mathbb{T}_{n}induced byNimue’s strategyζ\zetamust satisfy𝕋n⊆Hn\mathbb{T}_{n}\subseteq H_{n}; hence, we getΦe|ζ𝒰​(n)=ψn​[𝕋n×]⊆ψn​[Hn×]=Rn.\Phi_{e|\zeta}^{\mathcal{U}}(n)=\psi_{n}[{}^{\times}\!\mathbb{T}_{n}]\subseteq\psi_{n}[{}^{\times}\!H_{n}]=R_{n}.

Therefore, regardless ofζ\zeta, for anyy∈Φe|ζ𝒰​(n)y\in\Phi_{e|\zeta}^{\mathcal{U}}(n), we havey≤max⁡Rn=Ξe​(n)y\leq\max R_{n}=\Xi_{e}(n).
- •

By our assumptionF≤𝗍𝖶𝖦𝒰F\leq_{\sf tW}^{\sf G}\mathcal{U}viaee, there isζ\zetasuch thatΦe|ζ𝒰​(n)⊆F​(n)\Phi_{e|\zeta}^{\mathcal{U}}(n)\subseteq F(n).
SinceFFis nonempty, there is somey∈Φe|ζ𝒰​(n)⊆F​(n)y\in\Phi_{e|\zeta}^{\mathcal{U}}(n)\subseteq F(n); hence, we gety≤Ξe​(n)y\leq\Xi_{e}(n)andy∈F​(n)y\in F(n).

Consequently,φβ​(e):=Ξe\varphi_{\beta(e)}:=\Xi_{e}gives a total computable upper bound for a solution ofFF.

## Proof (Theorem5.2).

We show that Markov’s principleΣ1​-​𝖢1/ω\Sigma_{1}\text{-}{\sf C}_{1/\omega}has no total computable upper bound.
- •

Letg:ω→ωg\colon\omega\to\omegabe a total computable function.
- •

By the recursion theorem, there iseesuch that theΣ1\Sigma_{1}setSe={g​(e)+1}S_{e}=\{g(e)+1\}has an indexee.
- •

Then,e∈dom​(Σ1​-​𝖢1/ω)e\in{\rm dom}(\Sigma_{1}\text{-}{\sf C}_{1/\omega}), and for anyy≤g​(e)y\leq g(e), we havey∉Σ1​-​𝖢1/ω​(e)=Sey\not\in\Sigma_{1}\text{-}{\sf C}_{1/\omega}(e)=S_{e}; hence,ggdoes not give an upper bound.

Since the codomain of𝖢1/2{\sf C}_{1/2}is finite, by Lemma5.3, we obtainΣ1​-​𝖢1/ω≰𝗍𝖶𝖦𝖢1/2\Sigma_{1}\text{-}{\sf C}_{1/\omega}\not\leq_{\sf tW}^{\sf G}{\sf C}_{1/2}.
∎

The following combinatorial fact is easy to prove but quite useful.

## Fact 1(see[8, Proposition 2.9]and[24, Fact 4.4]).

LetTTbe a(n+1)(n+1)-fat finite tree.
Then, for any functionψ:T×→n\psi\colon{}^{\times}\!T\to n, there exists a22-fat subtreeU⊆TU\subseteq Tsuch thatψ\psiis constant on the setU×{}^{\times}\!Uof its leaves.

Using this fact, we show thatΣ1​-​𝖢k−1/k\Sigma_{1}\text{-}{\sf C}_{k-1/k}(linked to Markov’s principle𝖬𝖯k∨{\sf MP}^{\lor}_{k}) is not derivable from𝖢k/k+1{\sf C}_{k/k+1}(linked to𝖶𝖫𝖤𝖬k+1{{\sf WLEM}}_{k+1}).

## Theorem 5.4.

## Corollary 5.5.

## 6.Lawvere-Tierney Modality

We have completedcomputability-theoreticseparation proofs for various principles, but these are strictly computability-theoretic results; we havenotyet establishedlogicalseparations.
The focus from here on is how to translate computable separations into logical separations.
The key notion is a Lawvere-Tierney modality, and it is through this that logical separations are achieved in the subsequent sections.

For the basics of realizability topos theory, see van Oosten[37].
For general topos theory, see[27,17].

## 6.1.Tripos

Recall from Definition2.2that a potted setA¯\underline{A}is a pairA¯=(A−;A+)\underline{A}=(A_{-};A_{+})such thatA−⊆A+⊆ℕA_{-}\subseteq A_{+}\subseteq\mathbb{N}and0∈A+0\in A_{+}.
We sometimes calln∈A+n\in A_{+}ap.realizer, andn∈A−n\in A_{-}ana.realizer.
LetΩ\Omegabe the collection of all potted sets equipped with the following structure:

## Definition 6.1.

Heyting operations onΩ\Omegaare defined as follows:
- •

Top.⊤=(ℕ;ℕ)\top=(\mathbb{N};\mathbb{N}).
- •

Bottom.⊥=(∅;ℕ)\bot=(\emptyset;\mathbb{N}).
- •

Join.A¯×B¯=(A−×B−;A+×B+)\underline{A}\times\underline{B}=(A_{-}\times B_{-};\ A_{+}\times B_{+}).
- •

Meet.A¯+B¯=(A−+B−;A++B+)\underline{A}+\underline{B}=(A_{-}+B_{-};\ A_{+}+B_{+}).
- •

Implication.A¯⇾B¯=(A−⇾B−;(A−⇾B−)∩(A+⇾B+))\underline{A}\rightarrowtriangle\underline{B}=(A_{-}\rightarrowtriangle B_{-};\ (A_{-}\rightarrowtriangle B_{-})\cap(A_{+}\rightarrowtriangle B_{+})).

Here, defineA⇾B={e∈ω:∀n∈A.φe(n)↓∈B}A\rightarrowtriangle B=\{e\in\omega:\forall n\in A.\ \varphi_{e}(n){\downarrow}\in B\}forA,BA,B.
We assume that0is a code of a trivial total function (i.e.,idω{\rm id}_{\omega}), which ensures0∈(A−⇾B−)0\in(A_{-}\rightarrowtriangle B_{-}).
As usual,A⇾⊥A\rightarrowtriangle\botis abbreviated as¬A\neg A.

We also use the following set operations:

## Definition 6.2.

LetA¯,B¯,A¯i\underline{A},\underline{B},\underline{A}_{i}be potted sets.
- •

InclusionA¯⊆B¯\underline{A}\subseteq\underline{B}if and only ifA−⊆B−A_{-}\subseteq B_{-}andA+⊆B+A_{+}\subseteq B_{+}.
- •

Union.⋃i∈IA¯i=(⋃i∈I(Ai)−;⋃i∈I(Ai)+)\bigcup_{i\in I}\underline{A}_{i}=(\bigcup_{i\in I}(A_{i})_{-};\ \bigcup_{i\in I}(A_{i})_{+}).
- •

Intersection.⋂i∈IA¯i=(⋂i∈I(Ai)−;⋂i∈I(Ai)+)\bigcap_{i\in I}\underline{A}_{i}=(\bigcap_{i\in I}(A_{i})_{-};\ \bigcap_{i\in I}(A_{i})_{+}).

## Definition 6.3.

For each setXX, we call a functionφ:X→Ω\varphi\colon X\to\OmegaapredicateonXX.
By equipping with the pointwise Heyting operations, one can think ofΩX\Omega^{X}as a Heyting pre-algebra as well.
- •

We say that such a predicateφ\varphiisrealizableifφ​(x)\varphi(x)has a commona.realizer for anyx∈Xx\in X; that is,⋂x∈Xφ​(x)−\bigcap_{x\in X}\varphi(x)_{-}is nonempty.
- •

The set of all predicatesΩX\Omega^{X}onXXis equipped with therealizability preorder≤X\leq_{X}:φ≤Xψ⇔⋂x∈X(φ​(x)⇾ψ​(x))−≠∅.\varphi\leq_{X}\psi\iff\bigcap_{x\in X}(\varphi(x)\rightarrowtriangle\psi(x))_{-}\not=\emptyset.

That is,φ​(x)⇾ψ​(x)\varphi(x)\rightarrowtriangle\psi(x)is realizable.
- •

For a functionf:X→Yf\colon X\to Y, and a predicateφ∈ΩY\varphi\in\Omega^{Y}, thesubstitutionφ⋅f∈ΩX\varphi\cdot f\in\Omega^{X}is defined as(φ⋅f)​(x)=φ​(f​(x))(\varphi\cdot f)(x)=\varphi(f(x)).

## Remark.

By the above convention, ifφ\varphiis not realizable, then an index0of the trivial function realizes the negation¬φ\neg\varphi.

For preordered setsP,QP,Q, a functionα:P→Q\alpha\colon P\to Qis monotone ifx≤Pyx\leq_{P}yimpliesα​(x)≤Qα​(y)\alpha(x)\leq_{Q}\alpha(y).

Such a transformation is called aclosure transformation[37].
Hereafter,jXj_{X}is often abbreviated asjj.

## Observation 6.8.

Letjjbe¬¬\neg\neg-dense andφ\varphibe a formula.

## Remark.

A LT-modality/closure transformation can be regarded as an operation that transforms the truth values of predicates.
Its role here is to transform the semantics of acomputable universeinto the semantics of anoracle-relative computable universe.
In other words, a LT-modality serves the role of an oracle relativization[24,23,3].
This view is developed in Sections6.2and6.3.

## 6.2.LT-topologies as oracles

The key idea is that a LT-modality is an oracle.
There is a way to explain the link between a LT-modality and an oracle in abstract terms.
As we have already seen, a multi-query oracle computation is a free monadλ​V.μ​S.(∑x∈XSF​(x)+V)\lambda V.\mu S.(\sum_{x\in X}S^{F(x)}+V).
Similarly, a closure operator can also be understood as a monad.
Indeed, each oracle yields a “free LT-modality”jF(p)=μs.((∃x∈X.F(x)⇾s)∨p)j_{F}(p)=\mu s.((\exists x\in X.\ F(x)\rightarrowtriangle s)\lor p)and this is expected to provide a semantics for theFF-relative computable universe.
See also Ahman-Bauer[3].

Once we realize that the shape of a free LT-modality is identical to that of a free monad, we see that it can be described as an oracle computation.
To be explicit, for an oracleFF,jF​(P)j_{F}(P)can be described as the set of allcodes of strategies for solvingPPusing the oracleFF.
In the concrete analysis, it is precisely this explicit description that is important; for example, the structural analysis of the LT topologies on the effective topos has advanced rapidly and significantly thanks to the discovery of this explicit description[25,24,20].

While the above is a plausible abstract idea, unfortunately our category is not quite that perfect, so we set aside the abstract discussion for now and treat it simply as the underlying idea.
Let us actually get to work and verify that this idea works correctly even within the setting of total computability.

## Terminology.

Let us review the terminology related to a computation tree.
LetP¯\underline{P}be a potted set, andF:⊆X¯×Λ⇉YF\colon\!\!\subseteq\underline{X}\times\Lambda\rightrightarrows Ybe a potted bilayer problem.
Ap.computation tree of typeP+P_{+}is the following triple(Υ,ν,ψ)(\Upsilon,\nu,\psi):

- (2)

ν:Υ∘→X+\nu\colon{}^{\circ}\!\Upsilon\to X_{+}is a total labeling function on the internal nodes.
- (3)

ψ:Υ×→P+\psi\colon{}^{\times}\!\Upsilon\to P_{+}is a total labeling function on the leaves.

Ana.computation tree byFFis an(F;ν)(F;\nu)-branching subtree𝕋⊆Υ\mathbb{T}\subseteq\Upsilon; that is,
- (4)

The empty string belongs to𝕋\mathbb{T}.
- (5)

To each internal nodeσ∈𝕋∩Υ∘\sigma\in\mathbb{T}\cap{}^{\circ}\!\Upsilon, a queryν​(σ)∈X−\nu(\sigma)\in X_{-}is assigned, and{n∈Y:σ​⌢​n∈𝕋}∈F¯ν​(σ).\{n\in Y:\sigma\mbox{}^{\smallfrown}n\in\mathbb{T}\}\in\overline{F}_{\nu(\sigma)}.

In other words,
there is a secretα∈Λ\alpha\in\Lambdasuch that eachn∈F​(ν​(σ)|α)n\in F(\nu(\sigma)|\alpha)provides an immediate successor ofσ\sigma.

We say that ap.computation tree(Υ,ν,ψ)(\Upsilon,\nu,\psi)FF-solvesP−P_{-}if:
- (6)

There is an(F;ν)(F;\nu)-branching subtree𝕋⊆Υ\mathbb{T}\subseteq\Upsilonsuch thatψ(ρ)↓∈P−\psi(\rho){\downarrow}\in P_{-}for any leafρ∈𝕋×\rho\in{}^{\times}\!\mathbb{T}.

## 6.3.Sheaf subtopos

The structure consists of the truth-order onpredicates(ΩX,≤X)(\Omega^{X},\leq_{X})andsubstitutions(commonly called atripos[37]) merely provides a logical structure.
In particular, the logical structure of total computability used here is called theGrayson tripos.
A tripos, by itself, has no objects.
Therefore, we typically add the following kind of objects.

## Definition 6.14([36]).

AnΩ\Omega-setis a setXXequipped with an internal equivalence relation≈X:X2→Ω\approx_{X}\colon X^{2}\to\Omega; that is, reflexivity, symmetricity, transitivity are realizable.

## Example 6.15.

Define≈𝖭:ω2→Ω\approx_{\sf N}\colon\omega^{2}\to\Omegaas follows:(n≈𝖭m)={({n};ω)if​n=m,(∅;ω)if​n≠m.(n\approx_{\sf N}m)=\begin{cases}(\{n\};\omega)&\mbox{ if }n=m,\\
(\emptyset;\omega)&\mbox{ if }n\not=m.\end{cases}

Then𝖭:=(ω,≈𝖭){\sf N}:=(\omega,\approx_{\sf N})forms anΩ\Omega-set, called thenatural numbers object.

We make a brief comment here:
- •

For anΩ\Omega-setXX,(x≈Xx)(x\approx_{X}x)is treated as the set of allXX-codesofxx.
For example, the unique𝖭{\sf N}-code of a natural numbernnisnnitself.
- •

Inℕ\mathbb{N}, if the external equality==fails, then the internal equality≈𝖭\approx_{\sf N}cannot be realizable.
Such anΩ\Omega-set is usually called anassembly.
Therefore, an assembly has only information on codes.

## Fact 2.

The category ofΩ\Omega-sets and functional relations is a topos.

This is known as thetripos-to-topos construction.
In our setting, the resulting topos of total computability is known as theGrayson topos[36].
By adding a bit more structure to satisfy extensionality, one obtains the modified realizability tripos and the associated modified realizability topos.
However, within the scope of our discussion, there is no difference from the Grayson topos.
Therefore, in this article, we often refer to the Grayson topos as the modified realizability topos.

The interpretation of quantification in the category ofΩ\Omega-sets is given by:
- •

[[∃xX​φ​(x)]]=⋃x∈X[(x≈Xx)×[[φ​(x)]]][\hskip-1.4pt[{\exists x^{X}\varphi(x)}]\hskip-1.4pt]=\bigcup_{x\in X}[(x\approx_{X}x)\times[\hskip-1.4pt[{\varphi(x)}]\hskip-1.4pt]].
- •

[[∀xX​φ​(x)]]=⋂x∈X[(x≈Xx)⇾[[φ​(x)]]][\hskip-1.4pt[{\forall x^{X}\varphi(x)}]\hskip-1.4pt]=\bigcap_{x\in X}[(x\approx_{X}x)\rightarrowtriangle[\hskip-1.4pt[{\varphi(x)}]\hskip-1.4pt]].

If each quantification applies only to the natural numbers, and ifjjis¬¬\neg\neg-dense (i.e.,j​(p)−j(p)_{-}is nonempty for anypp), then the Kurodajj-translation can be concisely described as follows:
- •

[[∃xℕ​φ​(x)]]=⋃x∈ω[j​(x≈𝖭x)×[[φ​(x)]]j][\hskip-1.4pt[{\exists x^{\mathbb{N}}\varphi(x)}]\hskip-1.4pt]=\bigcup_{x\in\omega}[j(x\approx_{\sf N}x)\times[\hskip-1.4pt[{\varphi(x)}]\hskip-1.4pt]^{j}].
- •

[[∀xℕ​φ​(x)]]=⋂x∈ω[j​(x≈𝖭x)⇾j​([[φ​(x)]]j)][\hskip-1.4pt[{\forall x^{\mathbb{N}}\varphi(x)}]\hskip-1.4pt]=\bigcap_{x\in\omega}[j(x\approx_{\sf N}x)\rightarrowtriangle j([\hskip-1.4pt[{\varphi(x)}]\hskip-1.4pt]^{j})].

Thus,jj-realizability for arithmetical formulas can also be defined in the same way as before.
Note that here we distinguish between the symbols:ℕ\mathbb{N}is the type of natural numbers,𝖭\mathsf{N}is its interpretation in the category, andω\omegais the set of all meta-natural numbers.

## Remark.

It is known that a closure transformation on a tripos corresponds to a Lawvere-Tierney topology on the corresponding topos[37].
Furthermore, for each LT-topologyjj, there is a notion of ajj-sheaf, and the set of alljj-sheaves forms a subtopos of the original topos.
Then,jj-realizability provides a semantics for thejj-sheaf subtopos.
For computability-theoretic aspects of sheaves, see also[3].

Although this article does not explain the notion of a sheaf, we offer a few comments below regarding what the natural numbers object is and what its exponential object is, as these are necessary for interpreting second-order arithmetic.

## Example 6.16.

𝖭j:=(ω,j∘≈ℕ){\sf N}_{j}:=(\omega,j\circ{\approx_{\mathbb{N}}})is the natural numbers object in thejj-sheaf subtopos.
Ifj=jFj=j_{F}for some potted bilayer problemFF, this means:(n≈𝖭jm)={(F↘​{n};F+↘​ω)if​n=m,(∅;F+↘​ω)if​n≠m.(n\approx_{{\sf N}_{j}}m)=\begin{cases}(F^{\searrow}\{n\};F^{\searrow}_{+}\omega)&\mbox{ if }n=m,\\
(\emptyset;F^{\searrow}_{+}\omega)&\mbox{ if }n\not=m.\end{cases}

Using this, we can verify thatjj-realizability for the arithmetical formulas mentioned above provides the arithmetical semantics for thejj-sheaf subtopos.

Since the assemblies form an exponential ideal, the exponential object𝖭j𝖭j{\sf N}_{j}^{{\sf N}_{j}}in thejj-sheaf subtopos is also an assembly.

## 7.Realizability

## 7.1.MPand its variants

We now address the main topic of this article: the analysis of Markov’s principle and its weak variants.
Markov’s principle, also known as the principle of double negation elimination forΣ1\Sigma_{1}formulas, is formulated as follows.

## Definition 7.1.

Markov’s principle𝐌𝐏¯\underline{{\bf MP}}is the following principle:∀α∈2ℕ.(¬∀n∈ℕ.α(n)=0⟶∃n∈ℕα(n)≠0).\forall\alpha\in 2^{\mathbb{N}}.\ (\neg\forall n\in\mathbb{N}.\ \alpha(n)=0\ \longrightarrow\ \exists n\in\mathbb{N}\ \alpha(n)\not=0).

## Definition 7.2.

nn-disjunctive Markov’s principle𝐌𝐏¯n∨\underline{{\bf MP}}^{\lor}_{n}is the following principle:U​(α)\displaystyle U(\alpha)≡∀u,v∈n×ℕ.(α​(u)≠0∧α​(v)≠0)→u=v,\displaystyle\equiv\forall u,v\in n\times\mathbb{N}.\ (\alpha(u)\not=0\land\alpha(v)\not=0)\to u=v,E​(α)\displaystyle E(\alpha)≡¬∀u∈n×ℕ.α​(u)=0\displaystyle\equiv\neg\forall u\in n\times\mathbb{N}.\ \alpha(u)=0𝐌𝐏¯n∨\displaystyle\underline{{\bf MP}}^{\lor}_{n}≡∀α∈2n×ℕ.[U​(α)∧E​(α)⟶∃k<n​∀s∈ℕ​α​(k,s)=0].\displaystyle\equiv\forall\alpha\in 2^{n\times\mathbb{N}}.\ [U(\alpha)\land E(\alpha)\longrightarrow\exists k<n\forall s\in\mathbb{N}\ \alpha(k,s)=0].

## Notation.

For eachk,sk,s, putαk​(s)=α​(k,s)\alpha_{k}(s)=\alpha(k,s).
Ifα​(n)=0\alpha(n)=0for anyn∈ℕn\in\mathbb{N}, then we writeα=0∞\alpha=0^{\infty}.

U​(α)U(\alpha)is true if and only if it isFF-realizable, and such a realizer does not depend onα\alpha.

- •

Assume thatU​(α)U(\alpha)hasa.realizeraa.

As the underlying object is an assembly, this means thatu=vu=vis true.

Conversely, assume thatU​(α)U(\alpha)is true.
Ifu=vu=vis true, then a code ofuuis a realizer; so ifU​(α)U(\alpha)is true then the first projection which, given codes ofu,vu,v, returns a codeuugives ana.realizer.

It has been shown that this variant𝐌𝐏TM\mathbf{MP}_{\rm TM}is equivalent to the primitive recursive Markov’s principle𝐌𝐏PR\mathbf{MP}_{\rm PR}[10]:𝐌𝐏¯PR:∀e∈ℕ.(¬∀s∈ℕ.φe(0)[s]↑⟶∃s∈ℕ.φe(0)[s]↓)\underline{{\bf MP}}_{\rm PR}\colon\qquad\forall e\in\mathbb{N}.~(\neg\forall s\in\mathbb{N}.\ \varphi_{e}(0)[s]{\uparrow}\ \longrightarrow\ \exists s\in\mathbb{N}.\ \varphi_{e}(0)[s]{\downarrow})

Clearly,𝐌𝐏¯\underline{{\bf MP}}implies𝐌𝐏¯PR\underline{{\bf MP}}_{\rm PR}.

## 7.3.WLEMand its variants

The weak law of excluded middle𝐖𝐋𝐄𝐌¯\underline{{\bf WLEM}}states that one of two incompatible principles,AAand¬A\neg A, is false.
The principle𝐖𝐋𝐄𝐌¯n\underline{{\bf WLEM}}_{n}states that one ofnnmutually incompatible principles is false.

k\not=\ell\end{subarray}}A_{k}\land A_{\ell}\right)\longrightarrow\bigvee_{k<n}\neg A_{k}.

Ifn=ωn=\omega, then𝐖𝐋𝐄𝐌¯ω\underline{{\bf WLEM}}_{\omega}stands for the following principle:(¬∃k,ℓ∈ℕ.(k≠ℓ∧Ak∧Aℓ))⟶∃k∈ℕ¬Ak.\big(\neg\exists k,\ell\in\mathbb{N}.\ (k\not=\ell\land A_{k}\land A_{\ell})\big)\longrightarrow\exists k\in\mathbb{N}\ \neg A_{k}.

The weak law of excluded middle𝐖𝐋𝐄𝐌¯\underline{{\bf WLEM}}(=𝐖𝐋𝐄𝐌¯2=\underline{{\bf WLEM}}_{2}) is better known aspropositional de Morgan’s law¬(A∧B)→¬A∨¬B\neg(A\land B)\to\neg A\lor\neg B.
We next consider de Morgan’s law on the natural numbers, denoted by𝐃𝐌𝐋¯ℕ\underline{{\bf DML}}_{\mathbb{N}}:

## Definition 7.16.

## Remark.

In the effective topos, both𝖢1/2{\sf C}_{1/2}and𝖢1/ω{\sf C}_{1/\omega}are the strongest non-trivial oracles which induce the double negation topology.
In the total computable setting, we have seen in Theorem5.9that𝖢1/2<𝗍𝖶𝖦𝖢1/ω{\sf C}_{1/2}<_{\sf tW}^{\sf G}{\sf C}_{1/\omega}holds.
The question here is whether𝖢1/ω{\sf C}_{1/\omega}induces the double negation topology.
By Proposition4.7, one might expect𝖢1/ω{\sf C}_{1/\omega}to be the strongest non-trivial oracle; however:
- •

To realize¬¬A→A\neg\neg A\to A, even whenAAis nota.realizable, we must output anp.realizer forAA.
- •

However,(¬¬A→A)+(\neg\neg A\to A)_{+}does not preserve any information aboutA+A_{+}, and𝖢1/ω{\sf C}_{1/\omega}has no way to restore it.
- •

In general, an oracles with a fixedp.codomain, such as𝖢1/ω{\sf C}_{1/\omega}has no way to handle differentp.realizers for each distinctAA.

In fact, it seems correct to generally introduce oracles generated from logical principles asΩ\Omega-valued functions, which have no fixedp.codomain.

## 7.4.𝐂𝐓¯0\underline{{\bf CT}}_{0}and its variants

There is a previous work on the separation of strengths of the weak Markov principles using Kripke models[14].
However, unlike the Kripke model (the topological model), the realizability model automatically guarantees the validity of principles regarding computability.
Here, we consider some variants ofChurch’s thesis for constructivists.

First,𝐂𝐓¯0\underline{{\bf CT}}_{0}is a principle stating that any total multifunction is computable.

## Definition 7.18.

𝐂𝐓¯0\underline{{\bf CT}}_{0}is the following principle:∀x∈ℕ∃y∈ℕ.A(x,y)→∃e∈ℕ∀x∈ℕ.φe(x)↓∧A(x,φe(x))].\forall x\in\mathbb{N}\exists y\in\mathbb{N}.\ A(x,y)\to\exists e\in\mathbb{N}\forall x\in\mathbb{N}.\ {\varphi_{e}(x)\downarrow}\land A(x,\varphi_{e}(x))].

The principle obtained by replacing∃y\exists ywith∃!⁡y\exists!yis called𝐂𝐓¯0!\underline{{\bf CT}}_{0}!.

Next,𝐧𝐂𝐓¯0\underline{{\bf nCT}}_{0}is a principle stating that any partial multifunction is computable.

## Definition 7.19.

𝐧𝐂𝐓¯0\underline{{\bf nCT}}_{0}is the following principle:∀x∈ℕ.(¬D(x)→∃y∈ℕ.A(x,y))\displaystyle\forall x\in\mathbb{N}.\ (\neg D(x)\to\exists y\in\mathbb{N}.\ A(x,y))⟶∃e∈ℕ\displaystyle\longrightarrow\exists e\in\mathbb{N}∀x∈ℕ.[¬D​(x)→φe​(x)↓∧A​(x,φe​(x))].\displaystyle\forall x\in\mathbb{N}.\ [\neg D(x)\to{\varphi_{e}(x)\downarrow}\land A(x,\varphi_{e}(x))].

The principle obtained by replacing∃y\exists ywith∃!⁡y\exists!yis called𝐧𝐂𝐓¯0!\underline{{\bf nCT}}_{0}!.

Note that this is different from𝐄𝐂𝐓¯0\underline{{\bf ECT}}_{0}(extended Church’s thesis), which uses the notion of almost negativity; however, almost negativity performs well only for partial computability and is not a natural notion for total computability.

𝐂𝐓¯0\underline{{\bf CT}}_{0}also does not quite align well with the total computability setting, in that its realizer requires an unbounded search for haltingφe​(x)↓\varphi_{e}(x){\downarrow}for computations.
For this reason, we introduce the following weaker principle.

## Definition 7.20.

𝐰𝐂𝐓¯0\underline{{\bf wCT}}_{0}is the following principle111Note that this is different from what is commonly referred to as𝐖𝐂𝐓¯0\underline{{\bf WCT}}_{0}.
In𝐖𝐂𝐓¯0\underline{{\bf WCT}}_{0},¬¬\neg\negappears before∃e\exists e.:∀x∈ℕ​∃y∈ℕ.A​(x,y)→∃e∈ℕ​∀x∈ℕ.¬¬[φe​(x)↓∧A​(x,φe​(x))].\displaystyle\forall x\in\mathbb{N}\exists y\in\mathbb{N}.\ A(x,y)\to\exists e\in\mathbb{N}\forall x\in\mathbb{N}.\ \neg\neg[\varphi_{e}(x){\downarrow}\land A(x,\varphi_{e}(x))].

The principle obtained by replacing∃y\exists ywith∃!⁡y\exists!yis called𝐰𝐂𝐓¯0!\underline{{\bf wCT}}_{0}!.

## Definition 7.21.

𝐰𝐧𝐂𝐓¯0\underline{{\bf wnCT}}_{0}is the following principle:∀x∈ℕ.(¬D(x)→∃y∈ℕ.A(x,y))\displaystyle\forall x\in\mathbb{N}.\ (\neg D(x)\to\exists y\in\mathbb{N}.\ A(x,y))⟶∃e∈ℕ\displaystyle\longrightarrow\exists e\in\mathbb{N}∀x∈ℕ.[¬D​(x)→¬¬(φe​(x)↓∧A​(x,φe​(x)))].\displaystyle\forall x\in\mathbb{N}.\ [\neg D(x)\to\neg\neg({\varphi_{e}(x)\downarrow}\land A(x,\varphi_{e}(x)))].

The principle obtained by replacing∃y\exists ywith∃!⁡y\exists!yis called𝐰𝐧𝐂𝐓¯0!\underline{{\bf wnCT}}_{0}!.

Relativization to an oracle weaker than Markov’s principle preserves weak Church’s thesis.

## Theorem 7.22.

IfF≤𝗍𝖶𝖦Σ1​-​𝖢1/ωF\leq_{\sf tW}^{\sf G}\Sigma_{1}\text{-}{\sf C}_{1/\omega}then𝐰𝐧𝐂𝐓¯0\underline{{\bf wnCT}}_{0}isFF-realizable.

## Proof.

AssumeF≤𝗍𝖶𝖦G:=Σ1​-​𝖢1/ωF\leq_{\sf tW}^{\sf G}G:=\Sigma_{1}\text{-}{\sf C}_{1/\omega}.
For each indexee, we haveΦeF≤𝗍𝖶𝖦F≤𝗍𝖶𝖦G\Phi_{e}^{F}\leq_{\sf tW}^{\sf G}F\leq_{\sf tW}^{\sf G}G, so by transitivity (Proposition4.19), there is a total computable functionttsuch thatΦt​(e)G\Phi_{t(e)}^{G}refinesΦeF\Phi_{e}^{F}.

Realizer for𝐰𝐧𝐂𝐓¯0\underline{{\bf wnCT}}_{0}.Letaabe anFF-realizer for the premise∀xℕ​(¬D​(x)→∃yℕ​A​(x,y))\forall x^{\mathbb{N}}(\neg D(x)\to\exists y^{\mathbb{N}}A(x,y))of𝐰𝐧𝐂𝐓¯0\underline{{\bf wnCT}}_{0}.
As in Example6.20, itsFF-realizer is described as follows:
- •

Since the outmost connective¬D​(x)\neg D(x)is¬\neg, we only need to consider0as anp./a.realizer.
Thus,Φa+​(x,0)\Phi_{a}^{+}(x,0)yields a realizer for∃y​A​(x,y)\exists yA(x,y).
- •

Potential.For eachx∈ωx\in\omega, we haveΦa+​(x,0)↓\Phi^{+}_{a}(x,0){\downarrow}, and for any⟨y,z⟩∈Φa+​(x,0)\langle{y,z}\rangle\in\Phi^{+}_{a}(x,0),zzis ap.realizer forA​(x,y′)A(x,y^{\prime})for somey′y^{\prime}.
- •

Actual.For eachx∈ωx\in\omega, if¬D​(x)\neg D(x)is true, we haveΦaF​(x,0)↓\Phi^{F}_{a}(x,0){\downarrow}, and for any⟨y,z⟩∈ΦaF​(x,0)\langle{y,z}\rangle\in\Phi^{F}_{a}(x,0),zzis aFF-a.realizer forA​(x,y)A(x,y).

Regarding the conclusion of𝐰𝐧𝐂𝐓¯0\underline{{\bf wnCT}}_{0}, since the part following∃eℕ\exists e^{\mathbb{N}}starts with the negation, it suffices to provide an existential witnessee.

## 7.6.Majorization Versions of𝐂𝐓¯0\underline{{\bf CT}}_{0}

We introduce the principle𝐂𝐓¯0maj\underline{{\bf CT}}_{0}^{\rm maj}, which states that every arithmetically definable function is majorized by some computable function, as follows.

## 7.7.Independence of Premise𝐈𝐏¯\underline{{\bf IP}}

A prime example of a principle that does not hold in Kleene realizability (i.e., partial computable realizability), but does hold in modified realizability (a kind of total computable realizability) is the principle ofindependence of premise.

## 8.Future Works

First, regarding the logical direction:
Hendtlass-Lubarsky’s work[14]is notable for separating the strengths of the hierarchy of Markov’s principles while satisfying theaxiom of dependent choice𝖣𝖢{\sf DC}.
The trade-off is that their constructions are quite complicated.
One of our next goals is to separate the strengths of the hierarchy of Markov’s principles using simple realizability models while satisfying𝖣𝖢{\sf DC}.

Second, regarding the topos-theoretic direction:
it is natural to ask whether the effective topos coincides with the modified realizability topos augmented by Markov’s principle as an oracle, and whether the Lifschitz realizability topos coincides with the modified realizability topos augmented by the principle𝖬𝖯+𝖫𝖫𝖯𝖮{\sf MP}+{\sf LLPO}as an oracle.

## Question 1.

Is theΣ1​-​𝖢1/ω\Sigma_{1}\text{-}{\sf C}_{1/\omega}-sheaf subtopos of the Grayson topos (or the modified realizability topos) equivalent to the effective topos?

## Question 2.

Is the(Σ1​-​𝖢1/ω+Π1​-​𝖢≥1/2)(\Sigma_{1}\text{-}{\sf C}_{1/\omega}+\Pi_{1}\text{-}{\sf C}_{\geq 1/2})-sheaf subtopos of the Grayson topos (or the modified realizability topos) equivalent to the Lifshitz realizability topos?

Regarding the third direction:
As mentioned before, an oracle computability may be viewed as a free monad, and a LT-modality is a certain type of monad.
Meanwhile, there is existing research on the notion ofmonadic combinatory algebra(Cohen et al.[11]), which can be expected to relate to our work.
For instance, a relational combinatory algebra can be thought of as a combinatory algebra equipped with a multi-valued oracle.
As a specific example, the angelic computation model with “flip” may be related to the oracle𝖢1/2\mathsf{C}_{1/2}.
Cohen et al.[9]also present the demonic computation model with “flip” as an example that does not satisfy the axiom of countable choice𝖠𝖢ω{\sf AC}_{\omega}, while, in our context, we also have a vast number of oracle realizability models that fail to satisfy𝖠𝖢ω{\sf AC}_{\omega}; see[22]— in fact, most of realizability models based on (multi-valued) oracles that arise naturally in computable analysis and reverse mathematics refute𝖠𝖢ω{\sf AC}_{\omega}.

To what extent is a partial combinatory algebra (or its relative) endowed with a LT-modality related to a monadic combinatory algebra?
However, their work[11]considers monads on𝐒𝐞𝐭\mathbf{Set}, which is unsuitable for our setting.
Furthermore, their framework combines amonadMMon sets with anMM-modalityon propositions – offering perhaps greater customizability than ours, but resulting in a more complicated structure.
A task for future work is to identify a simple, concrete framework that clarifies the relationship between our research and theirs.
